PREFACE 

This laboratory manual was written to accompany the author's text-book entitled Inorganic Chemistry for Colleges, and is therefore designed for students who have had a preparatory or high-school course in General Chemistry. 

Having had long experience in teaching chemistry to students who offered the subject for entrance to college, as well as much experience on the College Entrance Examination Board, the author has learned that the knowledge of chemistry possessed by those who have had an elementary course, varies greatly. Some schools are adequately equipped for instruction, while others have limited facilities. Some students have done a minimum of experimental work, while others have had such thorough instruction that they can profitably take up the study of qualitative analysis after certain fundamental experiments in general chemistry have been performed.

To meet the needs of various colleges, universities, and technical schools, a fairly large number of experiments has been chosen which offer a wide variety, both in difficulty and in subject matter. The more elementary ones are designed for those who have had limited opportunity and facilities for laboratory work. Some of the experiments are of a quantitative nature and therefore provide for accurate work. 

The manual is divided into four parts, namely:

Part I. Introductory and the Non-metals. 

Part II.-The Metals. 

Part III.-Inorganic Preparations. 

Part IV. Carbon Compounds. 

The subject matter is flexible; therefore, the teacher can select the experiments which best meet the particular needs of his students.

vi 

PREFACE 

It is believed that the manual provides adequate material for six hours of laboratory work weekly for an academic year. 

If it be desired by the teacher to devote part of the first year in college to the study of qualitative analysis, a limited number of fundamental experiments may be assigned, such as 

Exercises 3, 5, 7-9; 11-25; and 27.

Some teachers will, no doubt, prefer to postpone a detailed study of qualitative analysis until sophomore year, and would, therefore, have plenty of time to cover Parts II and III, thus laying a solid foundation for analytical chemistry. Exercises 

26 and 37 provide for a considerable amount of practice in the identification of substances, while Part III (Inorganic Preparations) affords excellent laboratory experience in preparing substances. In general, small quantities of materials are used, thus making it possible to economize both in time and materials. 

Part IV (Carbon Compounds) provides material for those who wish to devote some of the time to the study of carbon compounds.

Most of the experiments contained in the manual have been subjected by innumerable students to the actual test of the laboratory.

Explanatory notes are given here and there to furnish the student with important information, to clear up some of the things which are not so apparent, and to save the time of the instructor. 

Much valuable matter is given in the Appendix. 

The author gratefully acknowledges his indebtedness for valuable suggestions to some of his colleagues, especially to Professor George A. Hulett, with whom he was at one time associated in conducting laboratory courses in General Chemistry, and to Dr. Everett S. Wallis, Assistant Professor of Chemistry.

The author is also deeply obligated to the Princeton University Press for generously allowing the use of material from his book entitled A Laboratory Manual in General Chemistry, also, to the Harvard University Press for the courtesy of allowing the use of certain exercises in Lamb's Laboratory Manual in General Chemistry, and to The Century Company.

REQUIREMENTS AND SUGGESTIONS 

1. When you report for the first laboratory exercise, read the directions on the apparatus list and then check off the articles. If any are missing or defective, apply to the storekeeper so that he may complete the list before you sign it and return it to him. 

2. See that your apparatus is clean and then arrange it in some definite order in your desk. Order and neatness are a great aid to success in laboratory work. 

It is well to have an apron to protect the clothing; and keep at hand a clean towel and a sponge. Always clean apparatus and return it to its proper place after it has been used. Wipe or brush up any materials which you may happen to spill.

The hoods must be kept clean and no solid matter thrown into the sinks. 

3. See that the reagent bottles are kept neat and in their proper places. The general reagents on the side-shelves are arranged in a definite order so as to avoid delay, and it is exceedingly important that you make yourself familiar with this arrangement and do your part in keeping everything in order. 

Whenever you have occasion to use a reagent in the liquid state or in solution, pour out the required amount into a beaker or test-tube, stopper the bottle, and return it to its proper place at once. In case the reagent is a solid substance transfer the amount required to a watch-glass or a piece of paper and return the bottle to its proper place. In no case remove a general reagent bottle to a desk or hood, and never return to the reagent bottle any reagent left over.

Unless otherwise instructed, use very small quantities of substances; usually 1 cc. of a solution is sufficient. 

Empty bottles are to be refilled at the storeroom. ix

REQUIREMENTS AND SUGGESTIONS 

4. The scales on the side tables are for weighing approximate amounts of materials and are always to be employed unless exact weights are called for. In weighing out solid substances protect the pans of the balance with pieces of paper of equal weight; or, in case exact weights are required, weigh out the substance on a watch glass. After the weighing has been completed put the balance in order and return the weights to their proper places. 

5. Apparatus taken from the storeroom on temporary order should be returned promptly after the experiment has been finished. 

6. Gas and water cost money; therefore, avoid waste. Use distilled water for solutions and for rinsing glassware; tap water may be used for other purposes. 

7. At the close of a laboratory period the desk and apparatus must be cleaned, everything put away in its proper place, and the desk locked. 

8. Before beginning an experiment, read it over carefully and provide everything required for carrying it through to completion. Be sure that the apparatus is set up properly, that the glass tubing is bent according to instructions, and that all joints are tight. Never resort to makeshifts. 

9. Experiments in which poisonous or disagreeable fumes or gases are given off must be conducted in the hood. 

10. The instructor in charge of the course will give detailed instructions as to the style of notebook required. Begin at the top of the page for each exercise, giving the date, object of the experiment, and the number of the exercise, preferably underlined. Give a brief description of the apparatus, with a diagram, and also the dimensions and volumes, in the metric system. Do not copy the laboratory procedure. Record the amounts of the reagents or other substances employed and, if they are not measured, estimate their weight or volume. If the experiment fails, state the reasons, if possible, and repeat it until the results are satisfactory. Observe the experiment carefully as it proceeds, note your observations, make all the explanations you can, and draw as many inferences as possible.

REQUIREMENTS AND SUGGESTIONS 

xi Always record observations, make explanations, and write the chemical equations whether asked for in the text or not. 

Write your notes in good English. In referring to a chemical substance use the full name with the symbol or formula after it. Thus, calcium carbonate, CaCO3; potassium permanganate, KMnO4; acetylene, C₂H2. 

Each student is required to work independently unless otherwise directed. All notes must be individual work and must not be written up before the laboratory work is done. 

The laboratory notebook must be written up and handed in for correction according to instructions given out by the instructor in charge of the course. 

11. An important part of your work will be to learn to use reference books (Appendix XV). Read the references in the 

Manual as carefully and completely as time will permit. Do not copy, but give the points in your own way. Endeavor to acquire the ability to read an article with such care that the main points can be given without again referring to the book. 

Learn to consult the Index, and look up answers to your questions as far as possible. You can readily find most of the information if you go about it in the proper way.

The reference books placed in the Laboratory should be consulted at the place provided and not taken to your desk.

TREATMENT IN CASE OF ACCIDENT Emergency Cabinet.-The contents of the emergency cabinet are to be used only in case of accident. In all serious cases, after rendering first aid, send or take the patient to the Infirmary.

Treatment of Burns.-Spread carron oil (an emulsion of linseed oil and limewater) upon a gauze bandage and lay directly upon the burned surface, holding it in place with a lightly bound bandage. Picric acid, in a 1-200 aqueous solution, can be used the same way; also, a saturated solution of magnesium sulfate.

If the patient has been burned with strong acid or alkali, the burn should be thoroughly washed with a large amount of water (a small amount might aggravate the injury), and the acid or the alkali then neutralized with sodium bicarbonate or boric acid, respectively. If the patient becomes faint from the shock, give aromatic spirits of ammonia, 1/4 teaspoonful in 1/2 glass of water. If the burn is slight, the zinc oxide ointment will be sufficient. 

Treat phosphorus burns with an aqueous solution of copper sulfate, and burns by bromine with a dilute solution of sodium bicarbonate and with alcohol.

Cuts and Wounds.-Wash out the wound with absorbent cotton that has been wet with 2 per cent aqueous solution of "mercurochrome," taking care that no cotton fiber remains in the wound. Remove all glass or other foreign matter with the forceps which have been sterilized in 50 per cent alcohol or in carbolic acid (1 to 20). If the cut is very deep, pour in some hydrogen peroxide. Unless bleeding is excessive, bring the sides of cut together, cover with a piece of gauze and apply strips of adhesive tape, then moisten the underlying gauze with mercurochrome solution. xiii

xiv 

TREATMENT IN CASE OF ACCIDENT To Control Excessive Bleeding.-Usually the application of cold water and holding the limb upright will be sufficient to stop the flow of blood. Bright red blood flowing in spurts is from an artery and may be controlled by tying a handkerchief around the limb, close to the wound and between it and the heart, placing a roll of gauze under the bandage directly over the artery and twisting the bandage with a stick until the flow of blood is stopped by compression. 

Care of the Eyes.-Acid chemicals should be immediately neutralized with a saturated solution of borax; alkaline liquids, with a half-saturated solution of boric acid, followed in either case by large amounts of water. Do not use ammonia or sodium bicarbonate in the eyes. The borax or boric acid may best be used in the eyecup, which is filled and inverted over the eye, and the patient made to wink the eyelids energetically.

Poisonous and Irritating Gases.-Get the patient to the nearest window and open it wide. If he is overcome, lay him upon his back, dash cold water upon his face and head and give 12 teaspoonful of aromatic spirits of ammonia in a little water. A cloth moistened with aromatic spirits of ammonia may be held under his nostrils.

Hydrocyanic Acid.-Give hydrogen peroxide in water. Keep patient flat and as warm as possible. Stimulate circulation by chafing feet and hands. Start artificial respiration at the first appearance of paralysis of muscles of respiration. Call a doctor. 

Chlorine and Bromine.—Administer alcohol or ammonia by inhalation. The liquid may be poured on a cloth. 

Hydrogen Sulfide.-Treat as described under hydrocyanic acid. 

Nitric Acid Vapors.-Give, by inhalation, ammonia and warm aqueous vapor.

Poisons.-Induce vomiting by tickling the throat with the finger, or give a gram of zinc sulfate in a glass of warm water. 

Then administer an antidote and follow it by about 50 cc. of olive oil.

Caustic Alkalies.-Give freely dilute organic acids, such as acetic. Follow by olive oil.

TREATMENT IN CASE OF ACCIDENT XV 

Silver Salts.-Give sodium chloride before emptying the stomach by vomiting. Repeat. Follow by soluble albumin in water and oil. 

Barium and Lead.-Give 10 grams of sodium sulfate in warm water.

Mercury and Copper.-Give quickly large and repeated doses of soluble albumin in warm water. Empty the stomach after each dose. Gargling with potassium chlorate will relieve the corrosion in the mouth and throat.

Arsenic.-Administer freshly precipitated ferric hydroxide or magnesium hydroxide, and then empty the stomach.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY EXERCISE 1 THE BUNSEN BURNER AND FLAME Structure of the Burner 

(a) The Bunsen burner allows air to enter at the side holes and mix with the gas before combustion takes place, the result being that more complete combustion occurs and a higher temperature is attained.

Connect the burner with the gas tap by means of rubber tubing, unscrew the barrel of the burner and examine each part. Turn on the gas, light it at the nipple and observe the flame. After turning off the gas put the parts of the burner together, turn on the gas and again light it. Observe the effect of closing and opening the airholes below. The Bunsen flame should extend 15 to 20 cm. above the top of the barrel and be colorless except for a small portion near the top of the flame. 

If the burner does not give such a flame, the nipple is defective. 

Ask the instructor to inspect it. 

Note. Do not be satisfied with a burner that gives a defective flame; for the success of many chemical operations depends upon securing the proper kind of flame. 1

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

Light, Heat, and Structure of the Flame 

(b) Close the valve at the base of the burner and light the gas. What is the color of the flame? 

Gradually open the valve and note the effect upon the flame. 

In which flame will a platinum wire glow more brightly? 

Which flame gives more light? Which more heat? (c) Hold a piece of clean porcelain both in the luminous and the nonluminous flame. A deposit of carbon (soot) is made in one of these flames. Can you burn the carbon off? (d) Rub together two small pieces of charcoal (carbon) so that some fine dust falls into the nonluminous flame. From the above experiments can you draw any inferences as to the cause of light and heat in flame? (e) Take a piece of narrow glass tubing about a foot long, hold one end of it in the inner cone of the burner, the tube being inclined, and see if you can light the gas at the other end of the tube. (f) Hold a piece of paper in the horizontal position and press it down upon the flame for an instant. (g) Press down a wire gauze upon the flame and note the effect. Turn off the gas, then turn it on again, and see if it is possible to ignite the gas above the gauze while none is burning below. (h) Turn off the gas, then turn it on and light it at the airholes below and allow it to burn a few minutes; observe the odor and carefully feel the burner. The Bunsen flame when turned too low often "strikes back" and burns below. This may lead to serious results if neglected (how?). (i) Make a sketch showing the various parts of the flame of the Bunsen burner. (R741.)¹ (j) Light a candle and while it quietly burns sketch the various parts of the flame. Does the ordinary gas flame differ essentially from the candle flame? (R741.) 

1 R in the Manual refers to the numbered sections in the author's Inorganic Chemistry for Colleges.

THE BUNSEN BURNER AND FLAME 

3 

The Reversibility of Flame (k) Arrange an apparatus as in Fig. 1. A large lamp chimney [Temporary order] is required for this experiment. 

Close the lower end of the chimney with a cork. Through one opening in the cork pass a tube for the introduction of illuminating gas, and through the other opening insert a short, straight brass or glass tube.

The upper end of the chimney is also closed with a cork bearing another straight tube, which is closed with a cork. Turn on the illuminating gas gently and after the air is displaced, ignite the gas as it issues from the small tube at the bottom of the chimney.

When the stopper is removed from the upper tube, the upward draft causes the flame of the burning gas to recede up the lower tube, and we have a flame produced by air (oxygen) burning in an atmosphere of coal gas. Now light the gas as it escapes from the tube at the top of the chimney. What are the products of combustion, assuming that hydrogen and carbon monoxide are the components of the gas? Do the products formed in the chimney differ from those formed outside the chimney?

EXERCISE 2 GLASSWORKING AND CONSTRUCTION OF A WASH BOTTLE 

In order to cut a small glass rod or tube, lay it on a desk or table, and, with a forward stroke of a sharp triangular file, make a fairly deep scratch across the glass. Grasp the glass in both hands, with the thumb on each side of the scratch, but on the opposite side from the latter. Now push gently with the thumbs and pull with the hands, and the glass will break where the scratch was made. (a) Cut a glass tube about 30 cm. in length and 5 or 6 mm. in external diameter into halves. If the glass does not break with slight pressure, do not exert much force but make the scratch deeper and try again.

Rounding off the Edges of Glass (Fire-Polishing) 

The edges of glass are sharp and very liable to cut the fingers as well as rubber connections and stoppers. To guard against this, always polish the edges very carefully by rotating the end of the glass in the flame until the edge is rounded off. (b) Fire-polish the edges of the tubes cut in the previous experiment. 

Bending Glass

In heating glass for bending, always use a wing-top burner, which gives a flat flame. Proper bends cannot be made by the use of the ordinary flame of the Bunsen burner.

To prevent the cracking of glass, never lay it while hot upon a cold surface such as that of slate or glass. It may be cooled, however, on asbestos. 

A tube may be bent as follows: 

Grasp the tube between the thumb and fingers of each hand,

CONSTRUCTION OF A WASH BOTTLE 

5 hold it lengthwise in the upper part of the flame and slowly and constantly rotate it backward and forward so as to secure uniform heating. When the tube has softened and will bend with ease, remove it entirely from the flame and slowly bend it into the desired angle, taking care that the parts of the tube on either side of the bend are in the proper plane. 

To anneal the glass tube and thus insure it against cracking, hold the bends, immediately after they are made, at the top 

FIG. 2. 

of the large yellow flame until they are covered with soot. The deposit of carbon will protect the glass from the air and allow it to cool slowly. When cool, the soot may be wiped off. 

A and B, Fig. 2, illustrate tubing incorrectly bent; C and D, correctly bent tubing.

To Bend a Tube into a Right Angle 

(c) Take one of the 15 cm. tubes of the previous experiment, hold it in not too large a flame until it softens, then remove it from the flame and bend it into a right angle as directed above.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

Do not be satisfied with a curve that is creased, flattened, or collapsed. 

To Bend a Tube into an Acute Angle To bend tubes into acute angles, they should be heated through about twice the space required for a right angle. (d) Take a piece of tubing 15-20 cm. long and about 5 mm. in external diameter, hold it in the flat flame and rotate it, at the same time moving it slowly to the right and left until about 6 cm. are uniformly heated. When sufficiently soft, remove it from the flame and bend it to the desired angle. 

Stirring Rods

Small glass rods (not tubes) 3-5 mm. in diameter and from 

10-20 cm. in length are very useful for stirring liquids and solids while they are being heated. (e) Take a piece of glass rod about 25 cm. long and heat it in the middle, using the ordinary flame of the Bunsen burner. 

When soft, remove it from the flame, draw it out slightly and allow it to cool. Cut it in two by making a slight scratch with a file and by applying gentle pressure. Round off all edges in the flame and preserve the rods.

Drawing and Sealing Tubes 

Small tubes are sealed by heating one end in the flame of the Bunsen burner, at the same time constantly rotating the tube to insure uniform heating. (f) Close one end of a tube about 10 cm. long and 5 mm. in diameter.

To draw out a small glass tube, hold the tube in the ordinary flame of the Bunsen burner at one spot until the glass has become quite soft. The tube should be constantly turned during the heating, and care should be exercised not to draw the two ends apart, but rather allow the glass to thicken at the heated spot. When the glass is quite soft, remove it from the flame and carefully draw out the tube to a narrow constriction by

CONSTRUCTION OF A WASH BOTTLE 

7 gently pulling the two ends apart. It should be constantly turned while it is being drawn out. Cut the tube at the middle of the constriction. (g) Make two dropping-tubes by taking a glass tube about 30 cm. in length and 5 mm. in diameter, heating it in the middle until quite soft and drawing it out, etc., as directed above. 

After cutting the tube in two, round off the edges in the flame, and preserve the tubes for future experiments, since they are very useful in adding a liquid drop by drop.

Ignition Tubes 

Ignition and combustion tubes are made of hard glass, which is infusible in the ordinary gas flame. Hard glass is used in all operations requiring intense heat.

In cutting hard glass the scratch must be made deeper, and in bending it the hottest part of the flat flame must be used. 

Unless it is heated and cooled very slowly it is apt to crack. (h) Select a piece of hard glass tubing about 20 cm. in length and 7-10 mm. in diameter and ask the instructor to show you how to make two ignition tubes from it. (It is necessary to use a blast-lamp for heating the glass.)

Boring Holes Through Cork¹ 

This is done by the use of a cork-borer. A set of cork-borers is made up of a number of brass tubes, sharp at one end, and of different diameters. The wire which accompanies a set of corkborers serves as a piston for the removal of the cylinder of cork which usually remains in the borer after the hole is made. 

To bore a hole successfully it is essential to have a sharp instrument. (i) Select a cork of the proper size and as free from cracks and channels as possible, and first soften it by the use of the cork-press. (The cork may also be softened by wrapping it in 

1 Rubber stoppers are cheap and may be substituted for corks. It is important, however, for the student to know how to bore holes through cork, for in certain operations in organic chemistry rubber cannot be used.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

paper to protect from dirt and then rolling it under the foot on the floor.)

Choose a sharp cork-borer slightly smaller than the tube which is to be inserted in the hole, hold the cork between the thumb and forefinger, press the larger end against a firm, soft board, or hold it in the hand, and slowly push the borer by a rotary movement so as to cut the cork, taking care to keep the borer perpendicular to the face of the cork. As soon as a slight projection can be seen on the under side of the cork, draw out the tube, and bore it from the opposite side so as to make the opening clean and with sharp edges. If the hole be too small, carefully enlarge it with a round file. 

Note.-Corks should be rolled and bored so as to fit without resorting to makeshifts, e.g., paraffin, for stopping leaks. 

Do not be satisfied with a poor cork and improperly bored holes; for this usually means failure in conducting the experiment and a great loss of time. Work thoroughly done will not have to be repeated. 

Always clean the cork-borer after use.

FIG. 3. 

Construction of a Wash Bottle 

(j) Wash a half-liter flask until it is clean. Select a good cork of the proper size and soften it by means of the cork-press. Bore two parallel holes through it and use a round file for smoothing or enlarging them. 

Bend two pieces of glass tubing as shown in Fig. 3, round off their edges in the flame and insert them into the holes of the cork. Make a nozzle by softening a piece of glass tubing in the flame and then drawing it to capillary dimensions. Cut off a nozzle 6-8 cm. long, round off the edges and connect it to the outlet tube by means of a short piece of rubber tubing. Sketch the apparatus.

CONSTRUCTION OF A WASH BOTTLE 

9 Before filling the flask with distilled water, have the instructor inspect it, and then test the apparatus to see that it is airtight.

Notes.-The apparatus may be tested for air-tightness as follows: 

Pour water into the flask until the lower end of the longer tube is well covered. Transfer the rubber connection to the shorter tube, then close the end of the rubber tube tightly with a clamp. Blow a few bubbles of air into the flask through the longer tube, and withdraw the mouth. If the apparatus is air-tight, water will be forced up the longer tube and will remain in an elevated position; if the water gradually falls, the apparatus is not air-tight.

In case a cork contains pores they may be often closed by thoroughly wetting the cork with water.

See that your wash bottle is kept clean and that it is used exclusively for the purpose for which it is intended. 

In slipping a rubber tube over the end of a glass tube or inserting a glass tube through the hole in a rubber stopper, always have the rubber wet, and exercise care not to lacerate the hands. 

In inserting a stopper in a flask, grasp the flask at the end of the neck where the stopper is to be inserted; for if the flask be held by the bottom, the insertion of the stopper may cause the thin glass to break and badly cut the hand.

Great care must also be exercised in inserting glass tubes through openings in corks.

EXERCISE 3 THE BALANCE AND WEIGHING 

(a) Examine carefully a chemical balance and state the mechanical principle involved. The balance is a lever of what class? Describe in your notes the following parts and state the function of each: post, beam, knife-edges, beam-stirrups, pans, pointer, and scale.

Describe the set of weights with your balance by giving the number of pieces and the denomination of each.

Slowly turn the milled head at the front of the balance, thus setting the beam and pans free to vibrate. Allow the beam of the balance to make several vibrations and observe the working of the different parts. Stop the swinging by slowly turning the milled head back to its first position. Try to do this just as the pointer passes the center of the zero mark on the scale, so that the motion will be stopped with the least possible shock to the mechanism.

Zero Point of the Empty Balance; The Sensibility of the Balance (b) The zero point of the empty balance is the point on the scale at which the pointer will come to rest from a free swing of the beam and pans. In practice it is possible to decide on this point without waiting for the swinging parts actually to come to rest. The beam is carefully released so that the pointer does not move off the scale, and then the magnitude of the swings, in scale divisions, is noted to the right and to the left of the middle point of the scale, making those to the left (—) and those to the right (+). The values are estimated as nearly 10

THE BALANCE AND WEIGHING 

11 as possible to 1/10 of a scale division. A record is made, with proper signs, of consecutive swings-say, three to the left and two to the right, or vice versa. The average of the swings on the left is added algebraically to the average of those on the right. The result is the "initial swing" of the balance, (-) if to left and (+) if to the right. The zero point is one-half of the initial swing.

Example.-¹A balance was set swinging and after one vibration on each side to insure against any possible jar on the release of the beam and pans, the following consecutive swings were 

L(-) 3.6 3.3 3.0 

Average, -3.3

R(+) 3.0 2.6 

Average, +2.8 -3.3+2.8 = −0.5, the "initial swing." -0.52——0.25, the zero point of the empty balance, i.e., the point on the scale at which the pointer would finally come to rest from a free swing of the beam and pans. What is the objection to using 2 and 2, or the same number of swings on each side of the balance? Is the experimenter limited to taking 3 and 2 swings, or may any even and odd combination of numbers be made? (c) The sensibility of the balance. The delicacy of the balance is determined by its sensibility, which may be defined as the displacement of the zero point, or point of rest, produced by a small difference of weight (say, 1 milligram) in one pan.

We have just determined the zero point of the empty balance (-0.25). An actual example will show the displacement, in scale divisions, produced by one milligram in the right-hand pan of the balance.2

Average, -8.5 +1.0 — 8.5 + 1 = −— 7.5; — 7.5 ÷ 2 = −3.75, the point of rest. 

As the zero point of the empty balance is -0.25, the displacement on the scale is 3.50 divisions, which is the sensibility of the balance with empty pans.

What would be the displacement on the scale if the point of rest were 3.75 and the zero point of the empty balance +0.25? Show this by drawing a diagram of the scale. 

Note.-The sensibility of a balance will vary more or less with various loads, so in making very accurate weighings it is customary to determine the sensibility for each load. (d) Determine the zero point of your empty balance and record it. Next, place a 10-milligram rider on 1 of the righthand arm (equivalent to 1 milligram in the pan), and determine the point of rest. What is the sensibility of your balance?

Now, place the rider on 2 of the arm of the balance and find the point of rest and the sensibility, as before. Weighing (e) A balance may be adjusted so that the zero point of the empty balance is the middle division of the scale; but the zero shifts with use, so for accurate work it is necessary to determine the zero and then determine the weight with this zero as the starting point. The object to be weighed is placed in the lefthand pan and the weights needed to counterbalance it are placed in the right-hand pan. Always have the beam arrested when removing or placing weights, or materials to be weighed, in the

THE BALANCE AND WEIGHING 

13 pans. In placing weights in the pans, begin with the larger ones and proceed successively to the smaller ones; after each change of weight a slight lowering of the beam arrest will indicate whether the weight is too heavy or too light. Proceed in this way until the weights are very near to the mass of the object being weighed; that is, until the difference is less than the value of the smallest weight. The balance case is now closed and the point of rest determined in the usual way. From this, the zero of the empty balance, and the sensibility of the balance you can get the weight of the object as accurately as the balance will weigh.

Example.-A body was placed on the left pan of the balance and it was found that the sum of the weights nearest to the mass of the object to be weighed was 7.443 g. 

The point of rest was determined as follows:

L(-) 3.6 3.3 3.0 

Average, -3.3

R(+) 4.4 4.0 +4.2 4.2 3.3 

+0.9; +0.9 ÷ 2 = +0.45, the point of rest.

From the zero point of the empty balance, the point of rest for 7.443 g., and the sensibility of the balance the exact weight of the object was found as follows: 

0.45 + 0.25 0.70 

7.443 g. were too light by 3.50 3.50 

0.2 mgr.

The weight of the object was, therefore, 7.4432 g. 

Explanation. The point of rest (+0.45) is to the right of the zero point (-0.25), i.e., there is too little weight on the right. Knowing the sensibility of the balance, we can calculate what additional weight must be added to 7.443 g. to give the correct weight of the object. The point of rest is 0.7 division (0.45 +0.25) from the zero point. Now, 1 mg. would cause a shift of 3.5 divisions (sensibility of the balance); therefore,

0.7 3.5 0.2 mg. 0.0002 g., and the weight of the object is 7.443+0.0002 

7.4432 g.

Notes. Great care must be exercised in using the balance. The following rules are to be observed: (1) The balance must be arrested every time an object or weight is placed on, or removed from, a pan. (2) The weights must be handled entirely with forceps and never touched with the hands. (3) Pieces of metal, glass vessels, crucibles, etc., if clean and dry, may be placed directly on the pan. All objects to be weighed should, when possible, be handled with forceps. Solids, salts, and liquids are to be weighed in a container (watch glass, crucible, beaker, or flask), which has been previously weighed or counterpoised. (4) Any object or mass to be weighed accurately must be at the temperature of the balance. Why? (5) A weighing consists of two observations. In many cases we have to weigh a vessel to which something is added, or from which something is removed, and what is required is the difference of the two weighings. 

In case these weighings can be made immediately after each other, it is not necessary to trouble about the zero of the empty balance. If these weighings cannot be made immediately, the zero point of the empty balance should be determined before or after each single weighing. 

If the beam of the balance remains at rest when released, it does not necessarily indicate that it is in equilibrium; it must always swing. After the final adjustment of weights has been made, count up the weights on the pan and then check this result by counting the vacant spaces in the box. 

This double count is necessary in order to avoid one of the most common errors in weighing.

Finally, record the result directly in your notebook and never on a loose slip of paper, which may be easily lost and thus necessitate the repetition of an entire experiment. (6) After completing a weighing, the weights and forceps must be returned to their proper places, any substance which has been spilled on the balance case, shelf or desk must be removed, and all left neat and in good order. 

Weighing a Crucible (f) In order to determine the exact weight of a crucible it is essential to have it clean, dry, and at room temperature when weighed. To accomplish this, the crucible is cleaned, wiped as

THE BALANCE AND WEIGHING 

15 dry as possible, supported in a pipestem triangle, heated for a short while in the flame of a Bunsen burner, and then, while still hot, transferred by means of forceps to a desiccator [Instructions] containing some granulated calcium chloride, which absorbs moisture. The crucible is allowed to stand in the covered desiccator until it comes to the temperature of the room (20 to 30 min.), after which it is weighed.

A desiccator is a short glass vessel fitted with an air-tight cover, and containing some desiccating agent, such as calcium chloride or concentrated sulfuric acid, above which is suspended or supported the material or body to be dried, or preserved from moisture. (See

Fig. 4.) 

Wash out your desiccator, if it is not already clean, wipe it with a towel until dry (do not heat it), cover the bottom with a layer of granulated calcium chloride 2 to 3 cm. deep, and then fit a pipestem triangle over the calcium chloride as a firm support for the crucible, taking care that the latter never comes in contact with the calcium chloride. Finally, spread a little vaseline over the outer part of the inside of the cover [Instructions] so as to make it fit tightly. The vessel is now ready for use.

FIG. 4. 

(g) Clean, dry, and heat a small porcelain crucible and cover; cool it in a desiccator and then weigh it to the third or fourth decimal place, depending on the sensibility of your balance, and record its weight. The crucible will be used in a subsequent experiment; therefore, keep it in the desiccator until required, and record its weight carefully in your notebook. Do not touch crucible or cover with the hands, but handle by means of forceps.

Note. With the cheap chemical balance employed in many laboratories of general chemistry, it is not possible to weigh accurately beyond the third decimal place (to milligrams). With a high-grade balance and care, the fourth decimal place (0.1 mg.) is significant.

EXERCISE 4 DENSITY, MASS, AND VOLUME 

"Quantity of matter" may be expressed in cubic centimeters or in grams. We generally measure solids by weight, liquids by weight or by volume, while gases are usually measured by volume. The ratio of mass to volume of any particular substance is called its density; that is, the density of any substance is the mass of 1 cc. of that substance and may be expressed by the formula, 1000B

where D = density, M = mass, and V = volume.

Density of Liquids 

(a) Thoroughly clean burette, and adjust it to stand as in Fig. 5. See that the stopcock is in good working order and then fill the burette with distilled water. Experiment with this instrument until you are able to allow the water to run out at any desired speed, or to stop the liquid at any desired mark. Make readings of the burette by noticing the division which corresponds with the lower meniscus of the liquids [Consult instructor]. Does the position of your eye make any difference in the reading? If so, why? Experiment until you can secure definite, reproducible readings. Describe your burette and state how accurately you think you can measure 10 cc. with it.

Density of Gases (Two students may work together) The density (absolute) of a gas may be found by determining the weight of a certain volume at the desired temperature, and then dividing the weight by the volume— D = The determination may be carried out in a heavy roundbottomed flask, fitted with a stopper and stopcock. The flask usually has a capacity of 250-300 cc. The air is first pumped out, the flask closed tightly, and then weighed. It is then filled with the pure dry gas at atmospheric pressure, the temperature and pressure noted, and the flask with its contents weighed. The increase gives the weight of the gas at the particular temperature and pressure. The volume of the flask is determined by filling it with water and reweighing. The

DENSITY, MASS, AND VOLUME 

19 grams of water correspond to the capacity of the flask in cc. 

The volume of the gas is now reduced by rule (R 48) to S.T.P., and the weight of the gas divided by its volume (in cc.) gives its normal density, i.e., at S.T.P. 

The relative density, or specific gravity, of a gas may be defined as the ratio between the weight of the substance and that of an equal volume of the standard gas when the latter is considered as unity. Oxygen, hydrogen, and air are used as standards.

The density or the specific gravity of a gas may be determined quite accurately as follows: (e) Procure an old-fashioned 25or 35-Watt electric light bulb, and by means of a sharp file make a scratch on the tip of the bulb so it can be broken off, leaving a small orifice, after the bulb has been weighed. Support the bulb in the upright position and then weigh it carefully. This gives, of course, the weight of the empty or evacuated bulb and the support. Break off the tip, saving all the glass. Air now rushes into the bulb and when it is full it is weighed, together with the tip of the bulb. The difference between this weight and the weight of the empty bulb gives the weight of a volume of air equal to that of the bulb, at atmospheric temperature and pressure, which should be noted.

The weight of an equal volume of carbon dioxide may be determined by running a capillary glass tube into the bulb, filling it with pure, dry carbon dioxide, and then weighing it, as before. (Repeat to be sure the weight is constant.)

We now have the weights of equal volumes of two gases, from which the specific gravity of carbon dioxide may be found (R 51).

In order to find the densities of the gases, it is necessary to know the volume of the flask. This may be accomplished with fair accuracy by filling the bulb with distilled water, a funnel with a capillary stem being employed, and then weighing it on 1 These so-called carbon filament bulbs may be purchased from dealers in chemical apparatus, and may be supported by soldering a metal collar to a piece of sheet metal.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

a rough balance. The number of grams of water may be taken as the volume of the bulb, in cubic centimeters. As seen above, the density of the gases may now be calculated. To determine the density at S.T.P., it is necessary to calculate the weight of the gas required to fill the bulb under standard conditions. 

Note. In case it is not feasible to procure bulbs for the individual student, the instructor may use this experiment as a lecture-room demonstration, a larger bulb being employed. 

In determining the density of a light gas such as hydrogen, it would be necessary to invert the bulb. 

What is meant by the mass of a body? Is it a constant or a variable quantity? Describe the unit of mass. Is it a fundamental or a derived unit? Explain. 

What is meant by the weight of a body? Is it a constant or a variable quantity? What is the relation between mass and weight?

Note.-The gram, the unit of mass, is the one-thousandth part of a mass of metal (platinum-iridium) known as the kilogram. The international prototype kilogram, like the Standard Meter, is kept at the International Bureau of Weights and Measures, near Paris. "National prototypes" of both standards are kept at the Bureau of Standards in Washington.

EXERCISE 6 OXYGEN. CATALYSIS Action of Heat upon Oxides 

(a) Heat in a hard glass test tube about 1 g. of each of the following substances: (1) mercuric oxide; (2) barium peroxide; (3) ferric oxide; (4) calcium oxide; (5) manganese dioxide.

The heating should be started slowly and the temperature gradually increased until the test is finished. The test tube should be supported in a nearly horizontal position and must be carefully cleaned after each experiment. Frequently test the gas in the tube for oxygen with a glowing splinter of wood. 

What changes, if any, do you observe in each substance?

Examine the residue in each case, find out its composition, and write any equations involved. How many of the above reactions may be reversed by varying the temperature? Can you give any reason as to why CaO, MgO and CuO are more stable than 

HgO, Ag₂O, and Au₂O?

Name a class of compounds besides oxides which yields oxygen when heated. Illustrate by writing equations. 

Note.-Clean the test tube by carefully heating in it a little nitric acid, and then rinsing with water. Test tubes, flasks, etc., may be quickly dried by carefully warming them in the Bunsen flame, inserting a piece of glass tubing long enough to reach to the bottom of the vessel, and then forcing through it a stream of air from a bellows.

When cool [desiccator], the chlorate is removed by means of the point of a clean file or knife-blade, roughly powdered, and brought into a clean dry

hard glass test tube and the tube and its contents carefully weighed. Procure a 250 cc. graduate and a large clamp from the storeroom [Temporary order]. Set up the apparatus represented in Fig. 8. Fill the graduate with water, invert it over a pneumatic trough, and clamp it in the vertical position, with the end under water. Clamp the test tube containing the potassium chlorate at an angle of about 45°, and see that the stopper with the delivery tube fits perfectly. The delivery tube is not to be over 5 mm. in diameter, and the end is to extend 28

VOLUME OF A MOL OF OXYGEN 

29 well up into the graduate, at least 5 cm. above the lower graduation mark.

Warm the tube above the chlorate, gradually bring the flame toward the end of the tube, and allow the generation of oxygen to go on very slowly. If the rate of decomposition of the salt is too rapid, some of the smoke (consisting of solid particles) which appears may be carried out of the test tube, the loss in weight will be too great, and the experiment will fail. Allow the generation of the gas to go on until nearly 250 cc. have been prepared, and the water in the graduate is 4 or 5 cm. below the end of the delivery tube. Remove the flame and allow the tube and contents to cool down to room temperature, keeping the apparatus assembled. While it is cooling, measure the atmospheric pressure and the temperature of the room and of the water in the pneumatic trough. (They should be the same. You want the temperature of the gas in the graduate.)

When the test tube is quite cool, adjust the graduate so that the water is at the same level within and without, and read the volume of gas. Weigh the test tube and contents exactly, as before. The loss in weight is the weight of the oxygen generated. Your object is to determine the volume of 32 g. (1 mol) of oxygen, measured dry at S.T.P.

Since the oxygen has been collected over water and is saturated with water vapor, which also exerts a measurable pressure (Table V), the barometric pressure, which is the same as that of the mixed gases in the graduate, is not the oxygen pressure; and as you are only concerned with the volume, temperature, and pressure of the oxygen, you therefore have an important correction which it is always necessary to make when a gas is collected over a volatile liquid. Read your textbook on "Mixed Gases" (R 49), and then calculate the volume occupied by 32 grams of pure dry oxygen, at S.T.P. This volume is known as a gram-molecular volume (R 184–185). 

What is the density of oxygen? 

What is the atomic weight of oxygen? How many atoms are there in a molecule of oxygen? of ozone? State reasons for your answer.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

Did the air which was in the test tube originally and which was carried over into the graduate, along with the oxygen, introduce an error? Give reasons for your answer.

Note.-Oxygen may be expelled from potassium chlorate at a much lower temperature by mixing with the carefully dried salt about the same weight of manganese dioxide, previously heated in a porcelain crucible six or seven minutes with the full heat of the Bunsen burner.

Allow the acid to interact with the metal until it is completely dissolved and then carefully run water through the funnel so as to displace all hydrogen from the flask but do not allow any air to be drawn in. The difference between the two weights will give the weight of water displaced by the hydrogen set free from hydrochloric acid by the weight of zinc taken in the experiment. LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

sults. Add a solution of calcium hydroxide to the third portion of the original solution. Explain. What are temporarily hard waters?

Note.-Hard soap is a mixture of the sodium salts of certain acids (stearic, palmitic, oleic, etc.) which may be obtained from fats. Soap forms with pure water a solution which is essentially colloidal; but in the presence of salts of calcium and magnesium the soap is decomposed and an insoluble curdy precipitate is formed, which is composed of calcium and magnesium salts of the fatty acids. No permanent lather can be obtained by adding soap to hard water until all the salts producing hardness have been thrown out of solution. (d) Shake vigorously a little finely powdered calcium sulfate with about 20 cc. of distilled water in a test tube; filter, and divide the filtrate into three portions, which are essentially permanently hard water (R 148). Test the first portion with a few drops of soap solution. Boil the second portion, and when it has cooled, test it in the same way. To the third portion add a few drops of sodium carbonate solution, boil, filter, and test the filtrate with a small amount of soap solution. Explain the results of the tests and write equations. 35 

Note. Taking sodium stearate as a representative soap and for the sake of simplicity representing the stearic acid radical (C17H3CO₂) by

St, the chemical change occurring when water containing calcium sulfate interacts with soap may be represented as follows:

CaSO.+2NaSt→ CaSt₂+ Na2SO4 (soluble). 

Sodium Calcium

Stearate Stearate Hydrates 

Experiments on hydrogen show that sodium interacts with water and liberates part of the hydrogen, forming a hydroxide; while iron liberates all the hydrogen from water, forming an oxide. Water is a very stable compound and will interact with some substances to form compounds, and without the loss of either hydrogen or oxygen. On decomposing these compounds, water is again obtained. The best examples of this fact are found in the case of certain substances which are crystallized

WATER AND HYDRATES 

41 from solution in water.

These solids, which contain the socalled "water of crystallization," are termed hydrates. (e) Crush a small quantity of copper sulfate crystals and heat the powder gently in a porcelain crucible until the blue color entirely disappears. Examine the residue, transfer it to a test tube, and dissolve it by heating with the least possible quantity of water [care]. Set the solution aside to cool and compare the deposit which is formed, with the original copper sulfate crystals. Does the crystalline form of these bodies depend on the presence of the water of hydration? (f) Carefully heat small quantities of the following substances separately in clean, dry test tubes, and note the results: (1) gypsum; (2) barium chloride; (3) potassium chloride (previously carefully dried); (4) magnesium sulfate; (5) potassium dichromate; (6) sodium dichromate. 

Do you think that all crystalline substances are hydrates? 

Can you think of any objection to the term, "water of crystallization"? Does the term seem to imply the presence of water as such in the crystals? Is there any evidence that this is so?

What evidence have we that hydrates are true chemical compounds? What is the difference, if any, between a hydrate and a hydroxide? Give three examples of each.

Solutions of cobalt chloride have been employed in the preparation of the so-called sympathetic inks. When the solution is dilute, the words which are written upon the paper are virtually invisible at first.

EXERCISE 12 MOLECULAR AND ATOMIC WEIGHTS The Molecular Weight of Carbon Dioxide 

(a) Clean and thoroughly dry a flask with a capacity of about 250 cc. and provide it with a tightly fitting stopper. 

Weigh the flask and stopper, which will equal the weight of the stoppered flask filled with air. Prepare carbon dioxide from marble and hydrochloric acid and wash the gas, first through water, to remove any HCl that may be carried over, then through concentrated sulfuric acid to remove moisture. Fill the flask with CO2 by downward displacement, then stopper it at once and weigh. Run in more CO2, cork, and weigh again. Repeat, if necessary, until the weight is constant. Record the pressure and temperature. 2 Allow the CO₂ to escape from the flask, fill it to the stopper with distilled water and weigh, thus determining the volume of the flask. The weight of the empty flask and its stopper are obtained by subtracting from the weight of the vessel filled with air the weight, under observed conditions, of a volume of air equal to the volume of the flask (1 liter of pure dry air at 

S.T.P. weighs 1.293 g.). The difference between this corrected weight and the flask filled with carbon dioxide is the weight of the latter. Reduce the volume of the CO₂ to S.T.P. and calculate the weight of 22.4 liters. This weight of the G.M.V. represents the molecular weight of the gas. (R 186.) What sources of error do you think this method of determining the density of a gas is liable to? Is it possible to determine the molecular weight of non-volatile and insoluble compounds?

EXERCISE 16 BASES, ACIDS, AND SALTS. VOLUMETRIC ANALYSIS 

Formation of a Salt by the Interaction of an Acid and a Base:

Neutralization 

(a) Pour about 10 cc. of a solution of sodium hydroxide into a small, clean evaporating dish and then add a few drops of neutral litmus solution. Add, drop by drop, dilute nitric acid, with stirring, until you get a change in the litmus. Now, add a little sodium hydroxide. Satisfy yourself that this color change may be reversed as often as desired. Finally, cause the litmus just to turn by the cautious addition of the proper reagent, and then evaporate the liquid to dryness on the steam bath and examine the residue. 

Note.-Neutralization gives rise to the formation of a salt along with water, and it presupposes a perfectly definite ratio between the base and acid. A most important application of neutralization is to the determination of the concentration of acids and bases. This requires standard solutions, or solutions of known concentration. The method of chemical measurement with standard solutions is known as volumetric analysis. 

This will be taken up in the next experiment. 

Write equations showing the interaction of barium hydroxide with nitric, sulfuric, phosphoric, and acetic acids; also, the interaction of ammonium hydroxide with the same acids. 

Neutralization and Volumetric Analysis 

To understand neutralization as it relates to volumetric analysis, it is first necessary to have a clear conception of what is meant by a normal solution. The following is a general 62

BASES, ACIDS. AND SALTS 

63 definition: A normal solution contains one gram-equivalent of the active substance in a liter.

The equivalent weight of an element is the quantity of the element which combines with (or displaces) 8 g. of oxygen or 

1.008 g. of hydrogen. (R 165.)

The equivalent weight of a compound is that amount of it which will interact with one equivalent of an element. 

A normal solution of a base (alkali) or of an acid contains one equivalent weight (gram-equivalent) of the base or of the acid in one liter of the solution. One liter of a normal alkali contains 17 grams (approximately) of available hydroxyl, and one liter of normal acid contains 1 gram (approximately) of available hydrogen.

What are the exact weights of hydroxyl and of hydrogen per liter?

Note. The preparation of solutions of acids and of bases of known strength is usually difficult, because acids and bases are, in general, substances which cannot be directly weighed. Can you think of reasons for this statement from the properties of the acids with which you are familiar? When we have, by any means, obtained a solution of an acid of known strength, we may with it determine the strength of a basic solution, and can then easily and accurately prepare solutions of other acids and bases of desired strengths. Indicators

Various indicators (R 248) are used in volumetric analysis. 

These are organic substances which take on a deep color in the presence of very small quantities of certain other substances, or change very sharply from one color to another. The most common indicators are the following litmus (aqueous solution), phenolphthalein (an alcoholic solution), methyl orange (aqueous solution), and congo red (aqueous solution). EXERCISE 17 OXIDATION AND REDUCTION 

Oxidation consists in the loss of electrons by atoms or ions; reduction is the converse, i.e., the addition of electrons. Thus, when a solution of ferrous chloride is treated with chlorine, ferric chloride is formed: 2FeCl2 + Cl2 2FeCl3.

From the electronic standpoint, a ferrous ion (Fe++) loses an electron, and a chlorine atom gains an electron, forming Cl-: or, 2Fe++ 2 → 2Fe+++, and Cl2 + 20→2C1-; 

2Fe+++ Cl2 → 2Fe++++ 2C1-. 

The two electrons lost by the two ferrous ions reduce a molecule of chlorine to the ionic condition; therefore, the equation can be balanced by taking one molecule of chlorine to two molecules of FeCl2. 

Fundamentally, then, reduction consists in the absorption of electrons by atoms or ions, while oxidation is the converse: Fe++ Oxidation

Fe++++. Reduction The reaction is called an oxidation-reduction reaction. (R 263.) Oxidation (a) Dissolve a small iron nail or tack in dilute hydrochloric acid and then add chlorine water to the solution. Write the equations showing the changes which occurred. How would you prepare anhydrous ferric chloride from iron wire? (R 696.) (b) Treat 10 cc. of a freshly prepared dilute solution of 67

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

ferrous sulfate with a few drops of a solution of potassium ferrocyanide. Write the equation. 

Repeat the test, substituting ferric chloride for ferrous sulfate, and write the equation.

Note.-Iron forms two series of compounds-ferrous and ferric. Fer rous compounds are transformed. by oxygen and oxidizing agents into ferric compounds. The former compounds are, therefore, active reducing agents.

Potassium ferrocyanide produces in solutions of ferrous salts, when air is completely excluded, a white precipitate. In neutral or acid solutions of ferric salts, an intense blue precipitate (Prussian blue) is formed (a test for a ferric compound). Prussian blue is the ferric salt of hydroferrocyanic acid, H4Fe(CN). (R 697)

Titration of Permanganate with a Ferrous Compound 

(Quantitative)

The strength of a permanganate solution may be readily and accurately determined by titrating it with a weighed quantity of ferrous ammonium sulfate, (NH4)2SO, FeSO, 6H₂O (Mohr's salt). This is a double salt. Molecular weight = 392.14.

Mohr's salt contains approximately one-seventh of its weight of iron in the ferrous condition. (1) Fill a clean burette with the solution of potassium permanganate from the vessel labeled "Permanganate for

Analysis.' Weigh out on a clean, dry watch glass exactly 

0.7 g. of Mohr's salt, introduce it into a flask or beaker without loss, dissolve in about 100 cc. of freshly boiled (why?) and cooled distilled water, and then add about 20 cc. of 6N sulfuric acid. Allow the permanganate solution to run in drop by drop until a point is reached when the rose color just appears permanently on shaking. Find the concentration of the permanganate.

Note. The oxidizing action of permanganic acid may be regarded as depending upon the splitting off of the anhydride manganese heptoxide (Mn2O7), which, in acid solution, is then decomposed into oxygen and manganous oxide. Two molecules of potassium permanganate furnish five atoms of "available " oxygen. Show that this is the case.

sulfate, and sulfuric acid (in excess) interact to form potassium sulfate, manganous sulfate, ferric sulfate, and water.

Estimation of Hydrogen Peroxide 

An aqueous solution of hydrogen peroxide reacts with potassium permanganate, in the presence of sulfuric acid, oxygen being liberated. (R 158.) (m) Put exactly 10 cc. of dilute hydrogen peroxide in a small beaker and acidify it with dilute sulfuric acid. Run in from a burette, drop by drop, with constant stirring, N/10 potassium permanganate until the solution is just pink. Repeat the experiment and take the average of your results. Calculate the strength of the solution of the peroxide. 

Note. Since 2 molecules of potassium permanganate are equivalent to 5 molecules of hydrogen peroxide, it follows that 

316 g. of KNnO, = 170 g. of H2O2. 

Therefore, 1 liter of N/10 KMnO4 = 1.7 g. of H2O2 and 1 cc. of N/10 KMnO4 = 0.0017 g. of H2O2. 

If, for example, 35 cc. of N/10 KMnO4 were required to interact with 

10 cc. of hydrogen peroxide, the strength of the peroxide solution in grams per liter is found as follows: 1000 0.0017 X 35 X or 5.95 g. 10

Sources of Chlorine 

(a) Place a small amount of each of the following substances in separate test tubes: Potassium chloride, potassium permanganate, barium oxide, barium peroxide, ferric oxide, manganese dioxide, and sodium peroxide. Add a little concentrated hydrochloric acid to each tube and place in the mouth of each a strip of moistened litmus paper. Warm these tubes, if necessary, and observe the changes. Which of these actions yield chlorine? What general conclusion can you draw? (R 271, III.)

Notes. It is best to make the above tests one at a time, or to have the tubes separated some distance from each other, so that the fumes from one tube may not influence the test being made in another tube. 

Free chlorine is readily detected by means of potassium iodide-starch paper. Iodine is liberated by the chlorine, and the former turns starch blue.

The litmus is bleached in the presence of free chlorine and water, but it turns red in the presence of acid (HCl).

Preparation of Iodine 

(0) Grind together in a mortar about 2 grams of sodium iodide or potassium iodide and an equal weight of manganese dioxide. EXERCISE 20 OXYGEN COMPOUNDS OF THE HALOGENS 

Hypochlorous Acid and Hypochlorites (a) (1) Add 2 or 3 drops of indigo solution to about 3 cc. of fresh chlorine water and note the change. Introduce a small piece of colored calico into a little chlorine water and allow it to stand for a short while and note any change that occurs. 

Note.-Hypochlorous acid, which is present in chlorine water, is a good oxidizing agent. When it decomposes into oxygen and hydrochloric acid, much heat is liberated. The solution oxidizes organic colored substances, such as indigo, producing colorless or less strongly colored substances. Indigo is oxidized to isatin, a yellowish compound: C16H10N2O2 + 2HOCl → 2C,H¿NO 2 + 2HCI. Add a little concentrated hydrochloric to a small crystal of potassium chlorate [Hood] and note the change. Are chlorides, e.g., KCl, affected by HCl? Note. The interaction of KCIO, and HCl is accompanied by the evolution of Cl2 as well as some CIO2 (chlorine dioxide, a yellow gas): 2KCIO3 + 4HC1→ 2KCl + Cl2 ↑ +2C102↑ + 2H₂O. 

When KCIO, is heated, two reactions take place simultaneously, which are represented by the following equations: (1) 4KCIO, → 3KCIO, + KC1, (2) 4KCIO, 2KCl +302↑ If the temperature is held just above the melting point of potassium chlorate, the change shown in equation (1) takes place. At higher temperatures oxygen gas is evolved freely and potassium chloride is formed. KCIO, may decompose as follows: KClO4 → KCl + 202. 

To secure as large a yield as possible of KClO4, heat KCIO, just enough to keep it melted and evolving oxygen slowly. SULFUR AND SULFIDES 

93 

Ammonium Sulfide

If a stream of hydrogen sulfide be conducted into an aqueous solution of ammonia until it is saturated, ammonium hydrosulfide, (NH) HS, is formed: NH¸+ H₂S→NH,HS. If this solution be mixed with an equal volume of "ammonia water, ammonium sulfide is formed: NH,HS+ NH,→ (NH4)2S; or, NH¸ 2NH OH + H₂S → (NH4)2S + 2H₂O. 

By dissolving sulfur in ammonium sulfide, yellow ammonium sulfide, (NH4)2S,, is formed; it is also called ammonium polysulfide, or ammonium persulfide. Both the colorless and yellow ammonium sulfide are very valuable reagents in analytical chemistry. (m) Take a few cc. of a solution of ferrous sulfate and add ammonium sulfide drop by drop until precipitation is complete. 

Try a similar experiment with copper nitrate.

Mix about 2 cc. of a solution of ferrous sulfate with the same amount of copper nitrate, acidify the solution with a few drops of dilute sulfuric acid, add 10 cc. of water, and pass in hydrogen sulfide until saturated (test?), heat just to boiling, then filter. Add ammonium hydroxide to the filtrate until the reaction is alkaline, and then a few drops of ammonium sulfide to complete the chemical change. Explain. 

To what use is ammonium sulfide put in analytical chemistry?

Note.-In (m) we learn the principle of separating one metal from another. Thus, copper in acid solution is precipitated by H2S as cupric sulfide. CuS is filtered off and the whole of the iron passes through the filter, and is precipitated as ferrous sulfide on adding ammonium hydroxide (to neutralize the free acid) and then ammonium sulfide. In other words, cupric sulfide is insoluble in dilute acids, while ferrous sulfide is soluble in acids. Ammonium sulfide is used, therefore, for precipitating metals whose sulfides are soluble in dilute acids. These include the metals of the iron group and the aluminium group. Why cannot Al and Cr be precipitated as sulfides by H2S or by (NH4)2S ?

If magnesium (more active than zinc) were treated with dilute nitric acid, would any hydrogen be evolved?

Note.-Nitric acid is an active oxidizing agent. When it gives up oxygen to a body, it is itself reduced. The reduction products depend upon conditions and upon the nature of the body oxidized. (R 390.) 

When non-metals such as sulfur and phosphorus are treated with nitric acid, nitrates are not formed, but the non-metals are oxidized and the acid is reduced.

When the metals in the electromotive series preceding hydrogen are treated with dilute nitric acid, hydrogen is displaced. If the metal is more active than zinc (e.g., Mg), a considerable part of the hydrogen escapes oxidation. In the case of zinc and less active metals, most or all of the hydrogen is oxidized to water. Write illustrative equations.

EXERCISE 25 CARBON AND SILICON. BORON 

Carbon (a) Place two or three small pieces of wood in a porcelain crucible, cover the wood with sand, and heat as long as the escaping gases burn. (R 427.) (b) Powder a little of the charcoal and heat it strongly on platinum foil. Does it burn? Try the same experiment with graphite. Will the diamond burn? Reduction on the Charcoal Stick (c) Many compounds containing metals, particularly compounds of silver, lead, copper, tin, iron, nickel, and cobalt, may be reduced and the free metals obtained by the use of a stick of charcoal. In order to prepare the little stick of charcoal, hold a crystal of sodium carbonate near the flame, and smear threefourths of the length of a stick of wood (e.g., a match with the head cut off) with the wet mass produced by warming the crystal. The stick is then slowly rotated in the flame which results in the formation of a crust of sodium carbonate on the carbonized wood. Heat in the fusing zone of the flame until the 106

CARBON AND SILICON. BORON 

107 crust is absorbed by the charcoal. This protects the stick from combustion. Take a very small portion of the substance to be tested and make it into a paste with a drop of melted sodium carbonate, and then take up a small mass of the paste on the end of the carbonized wood. Melt the mass in the lower oxidizing flame, and then move it through a portion of the dark cone into the opposite hottest part of the lower reducing flame. 

Effervescence of the sodium carbonate shows that reduction is taking place. In the course of a few minutes the action is stopped by placing the substance in the dark cone of the flame and allowing it to cool. By an examination of the end of the match, small, bright globules of certain of the metals may be seen. By cutting off the end of the carbonized wood and rubbing it up in a small mortar with water, the impurities may be washed away and certain of the metals obtained in sparkling fragments, which may be studied more minutely if so desired. (d) Prepare several sticks of charcoal as just described, then obtain on pieces of paper a very small amount of each of the following substances: AgNO3, PbO, CuO, SnO2, FeSO4, and

Sb2O3. 

Reduce the Bunsen flame to about 5 cm. height and regulate the air supply so as to obtain a luminous tip (upper reducing flame) inside the flame. Make a paste of a small quantity of each of the substances with sodium carbonate, and reduce on the charcoal stick. The charred end of the match is now broken off in a mortar, a little water added and the mass ground up with considerable pressure, the lighter particles of carbon, etc., being washed away. Examine the heavy residue in a good light, and submit your results to your instructor. (1) Silver, Lead, Tin, and Copper are malleable, and will show flat, shining discs. They may be identified by further tests. (2) Iron, Nickel, and Cobalt yield heavy gray particles, which are more or less magnetic. Iron is strongly magnetic.

Dry on pieces of filter paper the particles obtained by reducing the compound of iron, and test it with the blade of your knife magnetized by stroking it in one direction with one pole of a magnet.

EXERCISE 26 REACTIONS OF ANIONS; CLASSES OF ACIDS 

For the purpose of analysis, acids may be divided into groups which are based upon the solubility of their barium and silver salts. (R 468.) The tests are carried out in neutral solution with the reagents barium chloride and silver nitrate. Tests for some of the common acids are given below. 

The Sulfate Group 

(a) Sulfuric acid.-(1) Add barium chloride to a solution of sodium sulfate as long as a precipitate forms, then add a few cc. of dilute hydrochloric or nitric acid, and heat to boiling. (2) Repeat the experiment, using lead nitrate instead of the barium salt, and acidulate with nitric acid. (3) Test a solution of sodium sulfate with silver nitrate.

Note. In most cases a white finely divided precipitate insoluble in dilute hydrochloric or nitric acid indicates sulfuric acid. Fluosilicic acid (R 455) also forms with barium a white precipitate insoluble in HCl; but with potassium it yields a precipitate of potassium fluosilicate (distinction from sulfuric acid). The use of too much acid must be avoided; for it prevents the precipitation of small quantities of barium sulfate, and might possibly precipitate barium chloride. 

What are the properties of sulfate-ion? 

(b) Hydrofluoric acid.-Treat a solution of potassium fluoride with barium chloride and test the solubility of the precipitate in hydrochloric acid and in acetic acid.

Cover one side of a watch glass with a thin layer of paraffin and make some markings on it with a pointed instrument; mix a few grams of calcium fluoride in a lead dish with enough concentrated sulfuric acid to make a paste, cover the dish with 112

REACTIONS OF ANIONS; CLASSES OF ACIDS 113 

the watch glass, and keep it warm for half an hour, but do not allow the paraffin to melt. Now, melt off the paraffin and examine the glass for etchings. (R 302.)

Do the fluoride-ion and the silver-ion interact to form a precipitate? Look up and note the solubility of AgF; of CaF2 and of BaF2. What are the properties of fluoride-ion?

The Chloride Group 

(c) Hydrochloric acid.-Add a slight excess of silver nitrate solution to 2 or 3 cc. of a solution of sodium chloride and heat to boiling. Wash the precipitate by decantation, using hot water, and then shake it with dilute ammonium hydroxide. (R 530.) 

(d) Hydrocyanic acid [Poison!].-(1) Treat potassium cyanide solution with silver nitrate as in (c). Is the silver salt soluble in dilute ammonium hydroxide? Is it soluble in excess potassium cyanide? (R 530.) (2) Add a solution of ferrous sulfate to 1 or 2 cc. of a solution of KCN to which a few drops of a solution of NaOH have been added; then add a few drops of ferric chloride, heat the mixture a short while on the water bath, and then acidulate with hydrochloric acid. 

Note. In the presence of HCN, insoluble Prussian blue is formed:

FeSO4 + 6KCN = K.Fe(CN)6 + K2SO4; 3K4Fe(CN). + 4FeCl3 = Fe4(Fe(CN) 6) s↓ + 12KC1. (3) Take 1 or 2 cc. of a solution of KCN in a porcelain dish, add a few drops of yellow ammonium sulfide, and evaporate the mixture to dryness on the steam bath. Acidulate the residue with dilute hydrochloric acid, add a very little ferric chloride, and observe the coloration produced. 

Note. The cyanide is changed to thiocyanate, which reacts with the ferric salt to give the blood-red coloration (R 211): 

KCN+S (from the sulfide) → KCNS; 

3KCNS + FeCl, 3KCl + Fe(CNS) 3.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

(e) Hydrobromic acid.-(1) Add silver nitrate to a solution of a bromide and shake. (Compare with the test for HCl.) Is 

AgBr as soluble in dilute ammonium hydroxide as AgCl? Note its color. (2) Add to a solution of KBr enough chloroform to form a large drop at the bottom of the test tube; then add a few drops of chlorine water and shake the tube thoroughly. Explain from the ionic standpoint. 

Note.-Bromides are decomposed by free chlorine, and the chloroform dissolves the liberated bromine. (f) Hydriodic acid.-(1) Add silver nitrate to a solution of

KI, and compare the precipitate with AgCl and AgBr. Is it soluble in dilute ammonium hydroxide? Note its color. (2) To a solution of KI add enough chloroform to make a large drop in the bottom of the test tube; then add chlorine water, a drop or two at a time, and shake the tube vigorously. (Compare with a similar test for bromine.) 

Note. With excess chlorine, the iodine is oxidized to iodic acid, which is colorless. (3) To a very dilute solution of KI add dilute sulfuric acid, a solution of starch, then a drop or two of chlorine water, and shake. This is a very delicate test for iodine. 

(g) Hydrosulfuric acid (Hydrogen Sulfide).—(1) Add silver nitrate to a solution of sodium sulfide just neutralized by the addition of dilute nitric acid. Is Ag₂S soluble in dilute ammonium hydroxide? (2) Warm some FeS in a test tube with dilute HCl, and insert in the top of the tube a piece of paper moistened with a solution of lead acetate. REACTIONS OF ANIONS; CLASSES OF ACIDS 115 The Carbonate Group 

(h) Carbonic acid. (1) Add barium chloride to a solution of sodium carbonate. Is the precipitate soluble in nitric acid? (2) Test a solution of sodium carbonate with silver nitrate. 

Is pure silver carbonate formed? Explain. (R 257, [2].) (3) Pour a few cc. of a solution of sodium carbonate into a test tube, boil to expel air, and add dilute HCl. Hold a drop of calcium hydroxide solution on the end of a glass rod just above the surface of the liquid, taking care not to touch the wall of the tube, and observe whether it becomes turbid. 

Note. If in testing for carbonates the effervescence should be slight, or if warming is required, a cork bearing a bent tube must be put into the mouth of the test tube and the gas is expelled by boiling and passed through a second tube containing a little limewater. (i) Sulfurous acid.-(1) Test a solution of sodium sulfite with barium chloride. Is the precipitate soluble in dilute hydrochloric or nitric acid? Compare with sulfuric acid. (2) Prepare a strong solution of sodium sulfite, add sulfuric acid, warm, and note the odor of the gas evolved. with carbonic acid.) (Compare Note. Sulfites are readily oxidized to sulfates and a solution of a sulfite will usually give the test for sulfuric acid. This must be taken into account in testing for these acids. 

Is silver sulfite soluble in water? in nitric acid? (j) Silicic acid.-(1) Test a dilute solution of sodium silicate with barium chloride. Is the precipitate soluble in dilute nitric or hydrochloric acid? Compare with BaSO4, BaCO3, and

BaSO3.
