(2) To a few cc. of a solution of sodium silicate contained in a small evaporating dish, add HCl slowly with stirring, then evaporate on the steam bath to complete dryness; moisten the residue with strong HCl, warm, add water, and heat to boiling.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

Notes.-Solutions of alkali silicates are decomposed by acids. If a large quantity of HCl is added at once, the liberated silicic acid remains in colloidal solution; but if the acid be added drop by drop, with stirring, most of the acid separates in a gelatinous form.

Silicic acid may be rendered thoroughly insoluble by heating in an air bath to 120-135° for an hour or more; even at the temperature of the water bath the residue is rendered quite insoluble. (3) Make a bead of sodium metaphosphate (obtained by heating in the loop of a platinum wire microcosmic salt, or salt of phosphorus), take up a few small particles of silica or of a silicate, fuse thoroughly and cool and examine the bead.

Note.-Silica, or the silica in any silicate, is insoluble in a bead of sodium metaphosphate, giving rise to what is known as the "silica skeleton." (k) Boric acid.-(1) Test a fairly concentrated solution of borax with barium chloride.

Note.-The equation for the reaction may be written thus: Na2B4O7+ BaCl2 + 3H20 → 2NaCl + 2H,BO; + Ba(BO2)2 ↓. 

The precipitate is soluble in excess barium chloride.

3 (2) Place some powdered borax in a small porcelain dish, add a little strong sulfuric acid and then some alcohol; stir, warm the mixture on the bath, ignite it and observe the color of the flame.

Note. The color is observed best at the moment of igniting the alcohol. The green-bordered flame is due to the formation of the ethyl ester of boric acid. (3) Add just enough hydrochloric acid to acid reaction to a small portion of a solution of borax, dip a piece of turmeric paper into the solution, dry it and observe the color. (R 466.) 

Note.-Boric acid imparts a red tint to turmeric paper. Care must be taken to avoid the use of too much HCl, for it turns the paper brownish black.

An alkaline solution turns turmeric paper brown. This should not be mistaken for the red tint characteristic of boric acid.

REACTIONS OF ANIONS; CLASSES OF ACIDS 117 (1) Phosphoric acid.-(1) Test a solution of Na₂HPO with barium chloride. Is the precipitate soluble in dilute nitric acid? (Compare with BaSO,.) The precipitate is BaHPO,. Write the equation. 

(2) Test a solution of Na₂HPO by adding silver nitrate in slight excess. (R 413.) (3) Pour a little of a solution of Na₂HPO, into a test tube, add some ammonium chloride, and then add one-third the volume of ammonium hydroxide, followed by a little magnesia mixture; stir with a glass rod and allow it to stand. (R 413.) (4) Place 3 or 4 cc. of a solution of ammonium molybdate in a test tube and add to it not more than half as much of a solution of sodium phosphate acidulated with nitric acid, and shake and allow to stand, or warm very gently. 

Notes.-The yellow crystalline precipitate is ammonium phosphomolybdate. The following equation represents (approximately) the chemical change:

H,PO. + 12(NH4)2M0O, +21HNO, = (NH),PO₁· 12M003 ↓ + 

21NH4NO3 + 12H20.

Ammonium molybdate is used in excess, for this renders the precipitate less soluble. Ammonium nitrate and dilute nitric acid diminish its solubility and thus greatly facilitate the formation of the precipitate. 

The precipitate does not form in alkaline solution (being soluble in alkalies) and HCl and chlorides when present in considerable quantity render the test less delicate.

Orthoarsenic acid gives an analogous reaction with ammonium molybdate, with the difference that the arsenic compound is only formed quickly at the boiling temperature. 

(m) Arsenic acid.-(1) Test a solution of Na₂HASO, with barium chloride. Compare this reaction with (1) (1). (2) Place about 10 cc. of potassium or sodium arsenate in a test tube, make it just acid with nitric acid, add a few drops of silver nitrate solution, and pour somewhat dilute ammonium hydroxide solution carefully upon the top of it and observe the zone of precipitate which forms at the junction of the liquids.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

Note.-Silver nitrate precipitates from neutral solutions of arsenates chocolate-brown silver arsenate (difference from arsenious and phosphoric acids). The precipitate is soluble in acids and in ammonium hydroxide. (3) Add some ammonium hydroxide to a solution of an arsenate or arsenic acid, then pour in magnesia mixture, shake and allow it to stand.

Note. The precipitate is white, crystalline magnesium ammonium arsenate. (Compare with phosphoric acid.) Equation. (4) Take a few cc. of a solution of potassium or sodium arsenate, strongly acidulate it with hydrochloric acid, pass a rapid stream of hydrogen sulfide, and then heat. (R 630.)

Note. When a rapid stream of hydrogen sulfide is passed into a solution of an arsenate in the presence of strong hydrochloric or sulfuric acid, a yellow precipitate of arsenic pentasulfide is formed, even in the cold: 2K₂HASO4 + 4HCl + 5H₂S = 4KCl + 8H₂O + AS2S5 ↓ .

(2) Thoroughly shake a pinch of arsenic trioxide with half a test tube of water, and then filter the solution. Treat the filtrate with a slight excess of silver nitrate, and pour somewhat dilute ammonium hydroxide solution very carefully upon the top of it in such a way that the two liquids do not mix. 

Observe the zone that forms at the junction of the liquids. 

Note. Silver nitrate produces in neutral solution of arsenites a yellow precipitate of silver arsenite (compare with orthoarsenic acid). The precipitate is soluble in nitric acid and in excess ammonium hydroxide. 

At the junction of the liquids a neutral zone is formed, hence the appearance of the precipitate at that point. (3) Acidify a solution of sodium arsenite with dilute hydrochloric acid, and then pass in hydrogen sulfide. (R 630.)

Sodium; Preparation of Sodium Hydroxide (a) Weigh out about 10 grams of quicklime (calcium oxide), transfer it to a clean porcelain dish and slake it by adding a little water, using heat if necessary to start the action. After the quicklime has disintegrated, stir it with water enough to form a very thin paste ("milk of lime"). In a second porcelain dish dissolve 20 grams of anhydrous sodium carbonate in about 100 cc. of water. Heat the solution to boiling, stir the milk of lime thoroughly, and add it gradually, with constant stirring, to the solution of sodium carbonate; continue the boiling for a few minutes, transfer the mixture (after it has cooled somewhat) to a bottle, cork, and allow it to settle. When the supernatant liquid has become clear, draw it off by means of a glass siphon filled with water, or by decantation.

(g) (1) Heat a little potassium chloride on the end of a clean platinum wire and view the flame through the McCay "light filter" containing 31 g. of chrome alum dissolved in

NaCl. Explain. 

Note. The solution of chrome alum may be prepared in quantity by dissolving 310 g. of the crystallized alum in a liter of water by gentle heating. After the solution cools, it must be filtered. Ordinary wooden corks will serve as stoppers, and the solution will keep indefinitely.

The chrome alum filter absorbs completely not only the sodium light but that of lithium, strontium, calcium, and barium. The potassium flame rises from the glowing platinum wire in the form of a long, crimson, very bright streamer. This test is exceedingly delicate for potassium. 

The flames of rubidium and of cesium are also visible, but those elements are very rare.

Do not confuse the glowing end or loop of the platinum wire with the potassium flame, which must be in the form of a long, crimson streamer.

Analysis of Baking Powder (Qualitative) 

Baking powders are mixtures containing sodium bicarbonate (an acid salt) and an acid-forming substance, such as potassium bitartrate ("cream of tartar"), or calcium acid phosphate, or ammonium alum, or potassium bisulfate. Write the chemical formulas for these substances. Starch is added to delay the interaction of sodium bicarbonate and the acidic substance mixed with it. Explain. In the analysis of baking powders, the chemist tests for the radicals of the salts in the mixture and for starch. (m) (1) Place about 4 grams of baking powder in a small beaker, add 25 cc. of water and stir vigorously for several minutes. Filter and then test the filtrate, which must be clear, as instructed below. (2) Wash the residue on the filter paper two or three times with small portions of water, then puncture the paper and wash some of the residue into a test tube with about 10 cc. of water. 

Heat this suspension to boiling for a short while, allow it to cool, fill the test tube with water, shake, and then add one drop of iodine solution. A blue color indicates the presence of starch. (3) Pour 4 or 5 cc. of filtrate (1) into a small evaporating dish, add 5 drops of concentrated sulfuric acid and then evaporate to dryness [Hood]. Charring, accompanied by the odor of burnt sugar, indicates the presence of a tartrate. (4) Acidify about 5 cc. of the filtrate (1) with an equal volume of dilute hydrochloric acid and then add a small quantity of a solution of barium chloride. A white precipitate indicates the presence of a sulfate. (5) Add 1 cc. of filtrate (1) to 5 cc. of ammonium molybdate solution, acidify with HNO,, gently warm the mixture and set it aside, noting the formation of any precipitate. A yellow precipitate indicates the presence of a phosphate. (6) To about 5 cc. of filtrate (1), add an equal volume of sodium hydroxide solution and then heat to boiling. Note any

odor evolved and hold a piece of moist neutral litmus paper at the mouth of the test tube, but do not allow it to come into contact with the tube. Problems. (1) The molar solubility of silver acetate at 

16° C. is 0.0603, and conductivity measurements show that the fraction of the salt ionized is 0.71 Calculate (1) the solubilityproduct and (2) the ionization constant. (R 254 and 237.) (2) 100 cc. of water dissolve 1.49 g. of lead chloride at 18° C.

Find its solubility-product. In one liter of the solution there are 

14.9 g. of PbCl2. The molecular weight is 278; therefore, the solubility is 14.9/278 = 0.05 mol per liter. 0.05 molar (= 0.1 N) lead chloride is 72 per cent ionized; therefore the molar concentration of Pb++ 0.05 X 0.72 0.036, and that of Cl- 

0.036 X 2 = 0.072, since there are two chloride ions for every lead ion. The solubility-product is— KpbCl₂ (Pb++)(Cl−)² = (0.036) × (0.072)² = 0.000187. (3) 100 cc. of water at 18° C. dissolve 0.08 g. of lead iodide.

Assuming that a saturated solution of the salt is 80 per cent ionized, find its solubility-product. 

Determination of Chlorine (Present as Chloride) in Water 

Reagents 

(1) Standard Silver Nitrate Solution. 

It is prepared by dissolving 4.80 g. of pure AgNO, in distilled water and diluting to a liter. One cc. of this solution precipitates 0.001 g. of chloride. 

(2) Potassium Chromate. 

A solution of K₂CrO, is employed as an indicator. As soon as all the chloride radical in the water being analyzed has combined with silver to form AgCl (white) the least excess of silver (Ag+) will combine with the yellow chromate (CrO,) to form red, insoluble Ag₂CrO4, thus indicating the end of the reaction.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

No solid red Ag₂CrO, will be stable in the liquid before this point in the titration is arrived at, since this red silver chromate is sufficiently soluble to furnish a higher concentration of silver ions than can persist uncombined in presence of any notable concentration of chloride ions. Which solid is the more soluble, silver chloride or silver chromate? What can you say in regard to the degree of ionization of saturated solutions of these two salts? 

The indicator is prepared by dissolving about 2 g. of K₂Cro in 100 g. of distilled water. 

(n) Method of Procedure. 

Withdraw, by means of a clean pipette, 100 cc. of water from the jar labeled "Water for the Determination of Chlorine," and introduce it into a clean white porcelain dish. Add a very few drops of potassium chromate so as to give the water a faint yellow tinge, and add gradually from a burette, while constantly stirring with a glass rod, the standard solution of silver nitrate until the red silver chromate which forms after each addition of the nitrate solution ceases to disappear. The number of cc. of silver nitrate solution employed will express the chlorine present as chloride in parts in 100,000 of water.

If greater accuracy be desired, after completing a determination as just described, destroy the reddish tinge by adding a slight excess of a solution of sodium chloride, and repeat the determination on a fresh sample of water in a similar dish placed by the side of the former. By comparing the contents of the dishes the first tint of red in the second dish may be detected with great accuracy.

Why is aluminium hydroxide more soluble in sodium hydroxide than in ammonium hydroxide? 148

ALUMINIUM. TIN AND LEAD 

149 Notes. (1) Al+++ is precipitated by OH- as white gelatinous 

Al(OH)3, which has a tendency to form colloidal solutions. This is prevented by the presence of NH4Cl. (What effect has NH4Cl on the ionization of NH4OH?) (2) BaCO3 suspended in water precipitates aluminium as aluminium hydroxide mixed with basic salt: 2AIC1:+ 3BaCO3 + 3H202A1(OH);↓ + 3BaCl2 + 3CO₂↑. 

Barium carbonate in suspension (also solutions of alkali carbonates) contains OH- in consequence of hydrolysis. 

The carbonate has the action of a weak base; hence the formation of 

Al(OH), upon adding BaCO3 in suspension to a salt of aluminium. 

Barium carbonate suspended in water also precipitates chromium and ferric iron, but the bivalent metals in the iron group are not precipitated by it (important in analysis). Trivalent Arsenic 

(e) Boil a little arsenic trioxide with a solution of sodium hydroxide. Explain. (f) Boil a pinch of arsenic trioxide with 2 or 3 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid and dilute to 10 cc. Save the solution for experiment (g). Is arsenic trioxide an acidic or a basic oxide? Reasons for your answer. 

Note.-Oxides of elements such as arsenic and antimony may act either as acidic or basic oxides. Thus, when arsenic trioxide dissolves in sodium hydroxide, sodium arsenite and water are formed. In this case arsenic functions as a non-metallic element; that is, the oxide is acidic

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

in nature. (Compare this with the dissolving of carbon dioxide in a solution of sodium hydroxide.)

On the other hand, when arsenic trioxide dissolves in hydrochloric acid, arsenic trichloride (a salt of arsenic) and water are formed. Here arsenic functions as a metal; the oxide is, therefore, basic in nature. (g) Warm the solution obtained in (f) and pass hydrogen sulfide until the liquid is saturated with the gas. (What would be formed by dissolving the ppt. in yellow ammonium sulfide?) The compound of arsenic dissolves to form arsenic acid. Heat to expel excess chlorine, add ammonium hydroxide until alkaline, filter if necessary, and then add enough more ammonium hydroxide to form one-third the volume of the solution; add magnesia mixture, stir, and if no ppt. forms at once, allow it to stand. The ppt. is magnesium ammonium arsenate. (R 413.)

Notes. (1) The sulfides of arsenic and of antimony are soluble in yellow ammonium sulfide, (NH4)2Sx, or in colorless ammonium sulfide, (NH4)2S. They dissolve, forming salts of sulfo-, or thio-acids. Thus, As 2S3 dissolves in (NH4)2S to form ammonium thioarsenite, and in (NH4)2S to form ammonium thioarsenate:

(2) The salts of the thio-acids are decomposed by acids with the precipitation of the yellow sulfides of the metals: 2(NH) ASS3+ 6HCl → AS 2S3↓ + 6NH.Cl + 3H2S, 2(NH) ASS + 6HCl → As 2S+6NH4Cl + 3H ₂S. Note. The precipitation of arsenic as sulfide is not prevented by the presence of very great quantities of strong hydrochloric acid, nor are the sulfides of arsenic soluble in concentrated hydrochloric acid. The products obtained by passing H2S through solutions of arsenic acid or arsenates vary with conditions. On passing the gas through moderately acid solutions in the cold, no precipitate is at first formed (compare with the deportment of arsenious solutions); but after long-continued action of the gas, a mixture of As 2S5, As 2S3, and sulfur is precipitated. Determination of Arsenious Acid (Volumetric) 

The principle upon which the following method of analysis is based is the fact, that when arsenious acid is treated with

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

oxidizing agents such, for example, as the elements of the chlorine family, it is converted into arsenic acid:

H₁ASO3 + I2 + H₂O → H₂AsО、 + 2HI. 4 As a matter of fact, the determination is made in the presence of water and free alkali or an alkali salt, so the chemical change involved is substantially as follows:

As₂03 + 2Na₂0 + 21½ → As205 + 4NaI. 

The alkali must be present in sufficient quantity to combine with the HI formed.

In a similar manner a standard solution of arsenious acid may be used for the determination of iodine and other like substances.

This method of analysis is employed in the determination of "available" chlorine in bleaching powder, chlorine water, hypochlorites, etc., in solution, which is generally known as Chlorimetry. Reagents 

(1) Decinormal (N/10) Solution of Iodine. 

The 

This solution contains 12.692 g. of iodine per liter. iodine with about 18 g. of KI is dissolved in about 250 cc. of water and then made up to a liter. 

(2) Starch Suspension. 

To determine the end of the reaction involved in this method of analysis, an indicator is necessary. The best one for this purpose is starch, which produces with the slightest trace of free iodine in cold solution a blue color. 

(r) Method of Procedure. 

Withdraw exactly 10 cc. of the solution from the jar or bottle labeled "Arsenious Acid for Analysis" and introduce it into a clean porcelain dish or beaker and dilute it by the addition of distilled water. Add a little of the starch suspension and then run in gradually from a burette the decinormal iodine solution, with constant stirring, until the blue color just appears

permanently. Record the volume of the standard solution required and calculate the concentration of the solution of arsenious acid employed.

(t) Take 2 cc. of a solution of bismuth nitrate, dilute to 10 cc. with hydrogen sulfide water, warm, pass in H2S to complete precipitation, allow the ppt. to settle, decant, wash, then boil the ppt. with a very little dilute nitric acid. To the resulting solution add ammonium hydroxide to alkaline reaction, heat gently, filter, and wash the ppt. Dissolve by pouring a little strong hydrochloric acid through the filter, evaporate the solution to one or two drops, and pour into a small beaker of water. Explain.

Notes.-(1) The test for bismuth is more delicate the more completely the HCl is removed by evaporation. Time may be required for the separation of the BIOCI. 

(2) Bismuth forms a trivalent cation, Bi+++, which is very weakly basic in character. All of its salts are readily acted upon by water with the formation of sparingly soluble basic salts. This reaction is characteristic of bismuth. In many cases the resulting compounds are to be regarded as salts of the univalent ion BiO+, which is somewhat allied to the silver and mercurous ions. BiOCl resembles AgCl and HgCl (Hg₂Cl₂).

EXERCISE 34 CHROMIUM AND MANGANESE 

Chromium Chromium has a multiple valence and it may play the rôle either of a base-forming or an acid-forming element. There are several classes of chromium compounds, depending upon the state of oxidation of the element. (R 651.)

Chromous-ion, Cr++, has a blue color and is very unstable, being readily oxidized to the trivalent ion. Chromic-ion, Cr+++, has a violet color, and has properties very similar to those of aluminium-ion and ferric-ion. It has a great tendency to form complex compounds of all kinds, some of which are violet, some green. It forms a complex ion with ammonia, and these ions are reddish. With oxygen, chromium forms anions: CrO4, the yellow chromate-ion, and Cr₂O,, the orange-red dichromate-ion. 

These ions are formed by the oxidation of the cations of chromium.

Chromic Compounds: Chromic-Ion, Cr+++ (a) Add ammonium hydroxide to a solution of a chromic salt until slightly alkaline and shake. Now add considerable excess of ammonium hydroxide and shake again. The precipitate of chromic hydroxide is slightly soluble in the cold, imparting a peach-blossom color to the liquid. Equation. (b) Add sodium hydroxide to a solution of a chromic salt until the ppt. which forms goes into solution, and then heat to boiling for a short while. Explain. (c) Add ammonium chloride and ammonium hydroxide in. slight excess to a solution of a chromic salt, warm, and then add ammonium sulfide. Is the precipitate changed? Filter and 162

CHROMIUM AND MANGANESE 

163 wash. Mix the precipitate (after drying) in a mortar with three parts of dry sodium carbonate and one part of potassium nitrate or potassium chlorate, and fuse it on platinum foil. Boil the fused mass with water and filter, slightly acidify the filtrate with acetic acid and add lead acetate. Equations and explanations. (d) Carefully add a little solid sodium peroxide to a solution of a chromic salt, heat, and note the change. 

Notes.—(1) When the hydroxide-ion is brought in contact with the chromic-ion it forms a precipitate of chromium hydroxide, Cr(OH) 3. In case NH4OH is used in excess the precipitate is slightly soluble in the cold, owing to the formation of complex ammonia compounds. (2) Chromium hydroxide, like aluminium hydroxide, acts both as a weak acid and a weak base. As a weak acid it dissolves in the strong alkalies to form solutions containing alkaline chromites. On boiling the solutions of the chromites, hydrolysis takes place, resulting in the precipitation of chromium hydroxide (compare aluminium (R 586)):

Cr(OH)3 + 3NaOH NaзCrO3 + 3H20. (Give the explanation from the ionic standpoint.) (3) When chromic oxide and its compounds are mixed with sodium carbonate and sodium nitrate (or potassium chlorate) and the mixture fused, the chromium is oxidized to chromic anhydride, CrO3, which forms with the excess of alkali present yellow sodium chromate: Cr₂O3+2Na2CO3 + KClO3 → 2Na2CrO4 + KCl + 2CO2. 

Sodium peroxide also oxidizes chromic compounds to chromates. (e) Heat a particle of a chromium compound in a borax bead and note the change. Explain. (R 467.)

Chromic and Dichromic Acids and their Derivatives (f) Acidify 5 cc. of a solution of potassium chromate with acetic acid. What change occurs? Now add a solution of a lead salt. Explain fully. (R 658.) (g) Acidify strongly 5 cc. of a saturated solution of sodium or potassium dichromate with concentrated sulfuric acid [Care] and cool. Explain. (R 654.)

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

What is formed when a saturated solution of potassium chromate is treated with sulfuric acid? Equation. (h) Acidify the solution of potassium dichromate with hydrochloric acid, and pass in hydrogen sulfide until the change is complete. Add ammonia in excess.

Repeat the experiment, substituting sulfuric and sulfurous acids for HCl and H₂S. 

Note.-Chromium may function either as a metal (base-forming element) or as a non-metal (acid-forming element). In chromates and dichromates it functions as an acid-forming element. By the action of reducing agents on chromates, chromium is reduced and no longer functions as a non-metal, but as a metal (cation). Thus, by the action of hydrogen sulfide, potassium dichromate is changed into chromic chloride, -the chromium is reduced, and the color of the solution changes from orange-red to green:

K₂Cr₂07 + 8HCl + 3H ₂S → 2KCl + 2CrCl3 + 7H₂O + 3S ↓ . 

All salts of chromic acid give solutions which are yellow (the dichromates are orange-red); all salts in which chromium functions as a base give solutions which are violet or green. 

How may perchromic compounds be formed? (R 156.) Manganese

Manganese, like chromium and iron, has a multiple valence. 

It, therefore, forms a variety of compounds such as the manganous and the manganic, manganites, manganates, and the permanganates.

Manganese forms the pale pink manganous-ion (Mn++) and the unstable manganic-ion (Mn+++). The latter is weakly basic, the aqueous solutions of its salts being quickly hydrolyzed. It has a great tendency to pass into manganous-ion in acid solutions. With oxygen it forms two anions, manganate-ion (MnO), which is green and stable only in the presence of OH, and permanganate-ion (MnO,-) which is the purple ion characteristic of solutions of permanganates. Manganous-ion resembles in some respects the magnesium-ion and the ferrousion. In other words, the compounds of magnesium and of fer-

CHROMIUM AND MANGANESE 

165 rous iron are analogous to those of divalent manganese. (R 668 and 673.)

Manganous Compounds: Manganous-Ion, Mn++ (i) To a solution of a manganous salt add sodium hydroxide in excess, warm, filter, and expose the precipitate to the air. Explain. (j) Add ammonium chloride to a solution of a manganous salt, then ammonium hydroxide to alkaline reaction. What do you observe? Next add ammonium sulfide, warm, filter, wash the precipitate with water containing a few drops of ammonium sulfide (why?), dissolve the precipitate in cold dilute hydrochloric acid, boil to expel hydrogen sulfide, add NaOH in slight excess, then wash and dry the precipitate. (1) Test a bit of the precipitate with the borax bead in the oxidizing flame; also, in the reducing flame. (2) Take the main portion of the dry precipitate and rub it up in a mortar with 5 or 6 parts of anhydrous sodium carbonate, then fuse the mass on platinum foil until the mixture forms a thin liquid. (Previously clean the platinum foil by fusing sodium carbonate on it.) Explain. (k) Acidify a solution of a manganous salt with acetic acid and pass in hydrogen sulfide. What inference do you draw from this experiment? Is ZnS soluble in acetic acid? How would you separate Mn and Zn?

Notes. (1) The hydroxide-ion precipitates white manganous hydroxide, Mn(OH)2, which slowly absorbs oxygen from the air, forming brown manganous acid, H₂MnO,.

Manganous acid reacts with manganous hydroxide to form manganous manganite, which is the brown oxide Mn2O3. (2) The bivalent metals of the Iron group, if free from the sulfate-ion, are not precipitated by barium carbonate (see Aluminium). (3) If manganese compounds in a fine state of division be mixed with Na2CO3 and fused on platinum foil with access of air, sodium manganate is formed, which imparts a characteristic green color to the fused mass:

MnO + Na2CO3 + 02 → Na¿MnO4 + CO₂ ↑.

AgNO, per liter) with 10 cc. of a dilute solution of manganous sulfate (0.3 g. MnSO4 per liter), add a few drops of concentrated nitric acid, followed by about a gram of ammonium persulfate, (NH4)2S2O8; gently heat until a purplish color is obtained. Explain.

Note.-Manganous-ion, Mn++, is oxidized to permanganate-ion, MnO4. This is a most delicate test for manganese and serves as the basis for the colorimetric determination of small quantities of manganese in rocks. To insure the success of the experiment, sufficient silver nitrate must be present and the ammonium persulfate must be of good quality. (R 352.)

EXERCISE 35 THE IRON GROUP 

Iron forms a number of ions, some of the most important being the cations Fe++ and Fe+++, and the anions Fe(CN)--and Fe(CN)----. Name them. 

When iron dissolves in dilute hydrochloric or sulfuric acid, the ferrous-ion is formed; in nitric acid, the ferric-ion. 

Hydroxides of iron are weakly basic, and the salts derived therefrom are readily hydrolyzed.

Fe++ has properties somewhat analogous to those of Mg++ and Mn++. (Compare their salts.)

Fe+++ is colorless. Why, then, do aqueous solutions of ferric salts usually exhibit a yellow to brown-red color? 

Iron in Ferrous Compounds 

(a) Prepare a solution of a ferrous salt by dissolving a nail or some iron wire in dilute hydrochloric or sulfuric acid and test different portions of the solution with the following reagents: (1) Sodium hydroxide in slight excess; (2) Ammonium sulfide, first neutralizing the solution by addition of ammonium hydroxide; (3) Potassium ferrocyanide; (4) Potassium ferricyanide; 

(5) Potassium thiocyanate (KCNS). 

Note all you observe and write equations. (R 694–697.)

Notes. (1) The color of ferrous salts is usually light green or white, but on exposure to the air and by the action of oxidizing agents they are changed to ferric compounds. 167

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY. 

(2) Hydroxide-ion precipitates from solutions of pure ferrous salts, protected from all oxidizing agents, white ferrous hydroxide, Fe(OH)2. 

The precipitate, unless special precautions are taken to prevent oxidation of the ferrous salt, is dirty green in color. (3) With ferrous-ion, potassium ferrocyanide produces a white precipitate if all oxidizing agents be excluded. As ordinarily formed, the precipitate is bluish-white owing to the partial oxidation of the ferrous salt. (4) Fe++ with potassium ferricyanide produces a dark blue precipitate of ferrous ferricyanide, Fe:(Fe(CN) 6) 2 (“Turnbull's blue"). (5) Ferrous salts, when free from ferric salts, are not altered by potassium thiocyanate.

The ferrous chloride is oxidized to ferric chloride. Test a few drops of the solution on a watch glass with potassium thiocyanate. Test another small portion with potassium ferrocyanide. 

Notes. (1) Hydroxide-ion with ferric-ion forms a reddish-brown, gelatinous precipitate of ferric hydroxide. (2) Sulfide-ion does not precipitate iron as ferric sulfide: for this compound, which is formed only in the " dry way," is readily hydrolyzed in contact with water.

Ammonium sulfide produces with ferric salts a black precipitate of ferrous sulfide mixed with sulfur: 2FeCl3 + 3(NH4)2S → 6NH4Cl + 2FeS↓ +S↓. 

Hydrogen sulfide reduces solutions of ferric salts to the ferrous condition, with the liberation of sulfur: 2FeCl3 + H2S → 2FeCl2 + 2HCl + S↓. (3) By the action of oxidizing agents, ferrous compounds are readily

THE IRON GROUP 

169 converted into ferric compounds. Thus, ferrous chloride is changed by the action of nitric acid into ferric chloride: 3FeCl2 + HNO3 + 3HC1 →3FeCl3 + 2H2O + NO. (R 211.) (5) Ferric salts with potassium ferrocyanide produce a magnificent blue precipitate of ferric ferrocyanide, Fe,(Fe(CN) 6) 3 (“ Prussian blue "). EXERCISE 36 THE SEPARATION OF THE METALS (CATIONS) INTO GROUPS 

Wash out twenty-four test tubes and label them as follows: Ag, Al, As, Ba, Bi, Ca, Cd, Co, Cr, Cu, Fe, Hg(ous), Hg(ic), K, Mg, Mn, Na, (NH4), Ni, Pb, Sb, Sn, Sr, Zn.

Which of the metals are precipitated by hydrochloric acid?

(2) Pour into each tube containing a solution of a salt not belonging to the HCl group 5 cc. of a fresh solution of hydrogen sulfide. Name the metals which are not precipitated by any of the preceding group reagents.

Read over carefully the following definition of the groups of metals given and compare this with the observations you were able to make.

Definition of the Groups of Metals Group I. 

Silver Group.-Metals precipitated as chlorides (lead only partially) by hydrochloric acid: Ag, Pb, Hg¹. The precipitate may consist of: AgCl, PbCl₂, Hg₂Cl₂. 

Group II. Copper-Tin Group.-Metals precipitated by hydro gen sulfide in 0.3N acid solution; Copper Sub-Group: Hg, Bi, Pb, Cu, Cd (sulfides insoluble in yellow ammonium sulfide); and Tin Sub-Group: As, Sb, Sn (sulfides soluble in yellow ammonium sulfide).

The precipitate may consist of: HgS, Bi₂S, PbS, CuS, Cds, AS2S, AS2S5, Sb₂S3, Sb2S5, SnS, SnS2. 

Group III. Iron-Zinc Group.-Metals not precipitated by hydrogen sulfide in acid solution, but precipitated by ammonium sulfide: Fe, Cr, Al, Ni, Co, Mn, Zn.

The precipitate may consist of: FeS, Cr(OH)3, Al(OH)3, NiS, COS, MnS, ZnS.

Group IV. The Alkaline Earth Group.-Metals not precipi tated by sulfides, but precipitated by ammonium carbonate: Ba, Sr, Ca, (Mg).

The precipitate may consist of: BaCO,, SrCO, CaCO3 (Mg is not precipitated when sufficient NH4Cl is present). 

Group V. The Alkali Group.-Metals not precipitated by any of the preceding reagents: K, Na, (NH4).

Note.-Magnesium is often classified as a member of the Alkaline 

Earth Group, but in actual analysis it falls into the Alkali Group; that is, it is not precipitated as a carbonate along with carbonates of barium, calcium, and strontium, but passes into the last filtrate with K+, Na+, and (NH,*).

EXERCISE 37  

This experiment is designed to stimulate in the student's mind an interest in qualitative analysis and at the same time to offer a practical review of the tests for a large number of the more common cations and anions (or metals and acids). 

The compounds given to the student for identification should include both solids and water solutions and should be limited to the following ions:

Cations: Ag+, Pb++, Hg+, Hg++, Sn++, Sn++++, Sb+++, As+++, Bi+++, Cu++, Cd++, Al+++, Cr+++, Fe++, Fe+++, Mn++, Co++, Ni++, Zn++, Ba++, Sr++, Ca++, Mg++, K+, Na+, NH4+.

Anions: F, Cl-, Br-, I-, NO3-, NO₂-, S-, SO3-, SO4—, PO4, ASO3-, AsO4, AsО3, CO3-, BO3, C2H3O2¯, CrO4-. 

Ten compounds would be a fair number for a student to identify, and the speed with which he accomplishes it will be proportional to the knowledge he has acquired, the development of his powers of observation, and his adherence to systematic methods.

A brief but trustworthy record should be kept of the various observations which suggest the identity of the ions, and as soon as the compound is identified all the reactions which have taken place should be represented by equations. These should be written at the end of each record, in the order of the reactions. 

The instructor will give you unmarked or "unknown" compounds,-solid or solution. 176

Preliminary Examination 

(a) If a solid, proceed with the preliminary examination at least through (3). Use a very small amount for each test. 

1. Flame test with clean platinum wire. Yellow indicates sodium; violet (reddish through blue glass or the chrome alum light filter (Ex. 28 [g])) indicates potassium; red indicates strontium or calcium; green indicates barium or copper. 

2. Heat as hot as possible in a hard-glass tube.

Tests in Closed Tubes. 

Introduce a small amount of the substance, in the form of fine grains or powder, into a small glass tube sealed at one end. 

Incline the tube slightly and heat it gently at first, and finally to the highest temperature of the flame. The usual object is to ascertain the effect of the heat without oxidation. Note carefully any change in color or appearance as well as any odor. 

While heating the substance, apply a flame to the mouth of the tube and insert a glowing wood splinter or a piece of smouldering twine from time to time (why?). Also test any escaping gas with moist litmus paper (why?). The following are some of the results: Charring, change of color, fusion, decrepitation, evolution of water, escape of odorous and non-odorous vapors or gases, sublimates of various colors, magnetization. Water in the tube shows water of crystallization (or hydration) or water mechanically enclosed. Blackening indicates an organic compound. A colorless gas might indicate a sulfite, an ammonium compound, or a carbonate. (Note odor.) Violet fumes indicate an iodide. Brown fumes indicate a bromide, nitrate or nitrite.

Sublimate indicates a mercury or an ammonium compound. 

Heat, for preliminary practice, very small amounts of the following substances in closed tubes and note the result in each case: (1) cane sugar; (2) benzoic acid; (3) ammonium chloride; (4) iron pyrites; (5) albumin, horn or hide powder; (6) arsenic trioxide; (7) arsenic pyrites; (8) calcium oxalate; (9) lead monoxide; (10) zinc oxide.

In case of certain substances, e.g., sulfides, valuable informa-

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

tion may be obtained by heating the substances in open tubes.

The test is conducted as follows: 

Tests in Open Tubes. 

These tubes are open at both ends and are somewhat longer than the closed tubes. The purpose of the test is to bring the substance into contact with air at a high temperature in order to oxidize it. Many non-volatile substances interact with oxygen to form volatile or gaseous oxides. 

The substance should be in finely divided condition and it is placed near one end of the tube, which is inclined and brought nearer the vertical position as the temperature is raised, in order to increase the current of air. [Instruction.] 

Heat small quantities of the following substances in open tubes, note all that occurs, and write the chemical equations: (1) zinc; (2) iron pyrites; (3) galena; (4) cinnabar; (5) arsenic; (6) arsenic trisulfide. 

3. Is it soluble in water? If so, test with litmus paper; an alkaline reaction indicates a salt of a strong base. Acid reaction indicates a salt of a strong acid. (R 257.) 

4. Add hydrochloric acid (dil.). Effervescence, colorless gas, indicates carbonate, sulfite, or sulfide. Effervescence, brown fumes, indicates nitrite. 

5. Add sulfuric acid (conc.) Colorless gas indicates chloride, sulfide, sulfite, carbonate, or acetate. Brown fumes indicate nitrite, nitrate, or bromide. Purple fumes indicate iodide. 

6. Grind a little of it in a mortar with NaOH. Odor of ammonia indicates an ammonium compound. (b) If a water solution, take the following steps in regular order until you find the group of the cation, then proceed with the confirmatory tests until you identify it. Next, go to No. 7 and work in a similar way until you identify the anion. 

1. Test solution with litmus paper. 

2. Add dilute hydrochloric acid; a white precipitate indicates silver, mercurous mercury, or lead. 

3. Bubble hydrogen sulfide into the liquid which has been slightly acidified with hydrochloric acid. A yellow precipitate indicates cadmium, tin (stannic), or arsenic. A black precipi-

tate indicates copper, mercuric mercury, lead, or bismuth. A brown precipitate indicates tin (stannous). An orange precipitate indicates antimony. 

4. Boil the solution and add ammonium hydroxide to alkaline reaction, followed by ammonium sulfide. A black precipitate indicates ferrous iron, ferric iron, cobalt, or nickel. A white precipitate indicates zinc or aluminium. A grayish-green precipitate shows chromium. A pink precipitate indicates manganese. (The metals mentioned in [2] and [3] are supposed to be absent.) 

5. Boil the solution and add ammonium chloride, followed by ammonium carbonate solution. A white precipitate indicates barium, strontium, or calcium. 

6. With the above metals eliminated, it may be a magnesium, a sodium, a potassium, or an ammonium compound. To a small portion add hydrogen disodium phosphate. A white crystalline precipitate indicates magnesium. Make flame test; yellow indicates sodium. Violet (reddish with the chrome alum "light filter" or blue glass) shows potassium. Heat some of the original solution with excess of strong sodium hydroxide. 

Odor of ammonia shows an ammonium compound. 

7. (1) Add silver nitrate to the neutral solution. Precipitate indicates a chloride, iodide, bromide, phosphate, sulfide, carbonate, chromate, sulfite, borate, arsenite, arsenate, etc. (R 468 [II], [III].) (2) To the original solution add barium chloride solution.

A precipitate indicates a sulfate, phosphate, carbonate, chromate, 

(3) To original solution add dilute sulfuric acid. Effervescence indicates a carbonate, sulfite, nitrite, or sulfide. (4) To original solution add concentrated sulfuric acid; odor of vinegar indicates acetate. If acetate is not indicated, add a few drops of a saturated solution of ferrous sulfate, and a brown ring will appear if a nitrate is present.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY Confirmatory Tests; Positive Radicals (Cations) 

Note. As a rule, the confirmatory tests described below are made on solutions containing the positive radicals (cations). 

Lead. Add dilute hydrochloric acid; a white precipitate soluble in hot water shows the presence of lead. Add potassium chromate to the water solution containing acetic acid. 

Silver. Add dilute hydrochloric acid; a white curdy precipitate soluble in ammonia and insoluble in nitric acid shows silver. 

Mercurous Mercury.-Add dilute hydrochloric acid; a white precipitate which turns black upon addition of ammonia shows mercury in the mercurous condition.

Mercuric Mercury.-Dip copper wire into the solution; if it becomes silvered, mercury is present; and if the solution gives no precipitate with hydrochloric acid, the metal is in the mercuric condition. 

Copper. Add ammonia; a light blue precipitate which dissolves in excess reagent and gives a deep blue solution, shows the presence of copper. Copper also gives a green flame coloration. 

Cadmium. Add hydrogen sulfide; a light yellow precipitate insoluble in yellow ammonium sulfide shows cadmium.

Stannous Tin.-Add hydrogen sulfide to a slightly acid solution; a brown precipitate shows Sn++. SnS is soluble in yellow ammonium sulfide.

Stannic Tin.-Add hydrogen sulfide to slightly acid solution; a yellow precipitate soluble in ammonium sulfide shows stannic tin or arsenic.

When a solution of stannic salt is treated with NaOH or KOH, a voluminous, gelatinous, white ppt. is obtained, which dissolves in excess alkali. 

When a tin compound is heated in the reducing flame with soda on charcoal, metallic tin is obtained. 

Arsenic. Add hydrogen sulfide to solution strongly acidified with HC1; a yellow ppt. soluble in ammonium sulfide. 

The sulfides of arsenic form arsenic acid when boiled with conc. hydrochloric acid and a few crystals of KC1O,. Arsenic

acid may then be precipitated as magnesium ammonium arsenate as in Ex. 26. 

Antimony. Add hydrogen sulfide to slightly acid solution; an orange-red ppt. soluble in yellow ammonium sulfide. When a solution of most antimony compounds is poured into much water, a white ppt. is formed. (Ex. 33.)

Bismuth. Add hydrogen sulfide to slightly acid solution; a brownish-black ppt., insoluble in ammonium sulfide.

When a solution of a bismuth salt is poured into much water, a white ppt. is formed. (Ex. 33.) 

Aluminium.—Add ammonia; a colorless flocculent precipitate slightly soluble in excess reagent, but which returns on continued boiling after adding ammonium chloride, shows aluminium. 

Ferrous Iron. Add potassium ferricyanide; a dark blue precipitate shows ferrous iron. 

Ferric Iron.—Add potassium ferrocyanide; a dark blue precipitate shows ferric iron. 

Chromium. Add ammonia; a grayish-green, gelatinous ppt. is Cr(OH), (Ex. 34). When Cr(OH), is fused with Na2CO3 and KCIO, on platinum foil, a yellow product is formed. 3 

Cobalt. A borax bead turns blue when dipped in a cobalt solution, and then heated. Solutions of cobalt salts are pink. 

Nickel. Add sodium hydroxide; an apple green precipitate shows Ni. When the ppt. is heated on the borax bead in the oxidizing flame, it forms a reddish brown color. 

Zinc.-Add ammonium sulfide; a grayish-white precipitate shows zinc. 

Manganese. Add ammonium sulfide; a pink precipitate shows manganese.

Barium. If it gives a green flame coloration, add calcium sulfate solution; a white precipitate insoluble in hydrochloric acid shows barium.

Strontium.-If it gives a red flame coloration, add calcium sulfate solution; a white precipitate shows strontium. In case the solution is dilute, the precipitate will form slowly. 

Calcium. If it gives a red flame coloration and a white

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

precipitate with sodium carbonate solution, add calcium sulfate solution; no precipitate shows calcium. 

Magnesium. Add hydrochloric acid and then make the solution strongly alkaline with ammonia; next add sodium phosphate solution; a white crystalline precipitate shows magnesium. 

Potassium.-A violet flame coloration which becomes reddish through the chrome alum "light filter" (Ex. 28) shows potassium. 

Sodium.-Yellow flame coloration shows sodium.

Note. So many metals, due to the presence of sodium as an impurity, tend to color the flame yellow, that it is well to eliminate them by other tests before deciding that the yellow color is due to sodium. 

Ammonium. Add sodium hydroxide to the sample to be tested and heat. If the ammonium radical be present, the odor of ammonia will be evident. Acetate.

Negative Radicals (Anions) 

Add concentrated sulfuric acid; the odor of vinegar shows an acetate. 

Arsenate. Add silver nitrate; a chocolate-brown ppt. indicates an arsenate. (See, also, Ex. 26, m, [3], [4].) 

Arsenite. Add silver nitrate; a yellow ppt. indicates an arsenite or a phosphate. When a solution of an arsenite is acidified with HCl, and then treated with H₂S, a yellow ppt. (As,S) is obtained. Phosphates do not react thus. 

Borate. Add silver nitrate; a white ppt., turning brown on warming, indicates a borate. Confirmatory tests are given in 

Ex. 26, (k). 

Bromide. Add carbon disulfide and chlorine water, drop by drop, and shake; a yellow or red globule, according to the quantity of the element present, indicates a bromide. 

Carbonate. Add a dilute acid; effervescence of a gas which gives a white precipitate in limewater shows a carbonate or bicarbonate.

Bicarbonate.-Heat in a test tube; both water and carbon dioxide will escape if the substance is a bicarbonate.

Chloride. Add silver nitrate; a white precipitate insoluble in nitric acid, but soluble in ammonia, shows a chloride. 

Chromate. Solutions of chromates are yellow and give a yellow precipitate with barium chloride and with lead nitrate, insoluble in acetic acid. 

Fluoride. Add barium chloride; a white ppt. insoluble in acetic acid. Fluorides yield hydrofluoric acid which etches. 

Iodide. Add carbon disulfide and chlorine water, drop by drop, and shake the mixture; a purple globule shows an iodide. 

Nitrate. Add concentrated sulfuric acid and a little freshly prepared ferrous sulfate (saturated solution); a brown ring between the liquids shows a nitrate. 

Nitrite. Add dilute sulfuric acid; red-brown fumes show a nitrite. 

Phosphate. Add hydrochloric acid and make alkaline with ammonia, then add magnesium chloride solution; a white crystalline precipitate shows a phosphate. (See Ex. 26, [1].)

Sulfate. Add barium chloride solution; a white precipitate insoluble in dilute hydrochloric acid shows a sulfate. 

Sulfide. Add dilute acid; the odor of rotten eggs shows a sulfide. Also, hydrogen sulfide gas blackens lead acetate paper.

Sulfite. Add dilute acid; the odor of burning sulfur indicates a sulfite. 

Note. When a thiosulfate is treated with an acid, sulfur dioxide and sulfur are liberated. (R 351.)

EXERCISE 38 PREPARATION OF INSOLUBLE AND SOLUBLE SALTS 

This experiment awakens an interest in constructive work, gives a review of the solubility rules, and shows the practical value of a knowledge of chemical arithmetic. To the mind of the careful student it offers convincing proof of the definiteness of chemical combinations. 

An Insoluble Compound 

(a) Prepare a definite number of grams of one of these compounds: (1) Lead sulfate, (2) Lead chromate, 

(3) Strontium sulfate, 

(4) Barium sulfate, (5) Silver chromate, (6) Mercuric iodide, (7) Mercurous chloride, (8) Ferric ferrocyanide

The salts may be formed by mixing solutions of the following salts: 

(1) Lead nitrate ferrous sulfate → 

(2) Lead nitrate + potassium chromate → (3) Strontium nitrate + copper sulfate → (4) Barium chloride + zinc sulfate → (5) Silver nitrate + potassium chromate → (6) Mercuric chloride + potassium iodide → (7) Mercurous nitrate + sodium chloride → (8) Ferric sulfate + potassium ferrocyanide → 184

INSOLUBLE AND SOLUBLE SALTS 

185 

Procedure. Write the equation and calculate the exact number of grams of each of the two compounds required. 

Note. Always take into account water of hydration in calculating weights.

The solubility, in round numbers, of the anhydrous salts in grams per 100 cc. of water at 20° C. is as follows:

Pb(NO3)2... 54 FeSO4.. Sr(NO3)2. 71 K2CrO4. BaCl2. 36 CuSO4. AgNO3.. 222 ZnSO4. HgCl2. 74 KI. 

HgNO3, very soluble in water. 

Fe2(SO4)3, very soluble. NaCl.. 26 63 21 55 144 36 25 K4Fe(CN)6. 

In case the salt is a hydrate the weight required to prepare a saturated solution may be quickly calculated. To illustrate, barium chloride crystallizes as BaCl2 2H2O, which has a molecular weight of 244.4, while BaCl2 has a molecular weight of 208.4. The weight of BaCl2 2H2O is calculated as follows: 

208.4 36 244.4 Weigh the compounds carefully and dissolve them separately in just enough water to make a saturated solution at room temperature (20° C.). If you heat them to hasten solution, add water to offset the amount that has evaporated. (This can be determined by measuring in a graduated cylinder, or by marking the height of liquid in the beaker with a label.) 

Filter the solutions and pour one solution into the other, stirring the mixture constantly. 

Prepare a large filter, then add water to your mixture and stir; let the precipitate settle, and decant the wash water into your filter. Add more water, with stirring, and then pour the precipitate and all into the filter. Let it drain, and keep pouring water through the filter until a test of the filtrate does not show the presence of the anion of the soluble compound formed. 

After it is thoroughly drained remove the filter paper and spread it out on a glass plate to dry. When it is entirely dry,

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

weigh it, allowing for the weight of the filter paper, and compare its weight with the calculated weight. 

Soluble Salts

Preliminary. Soluble salts may be prepared by the precipitation method, but the need of washing the precipitate thoroughly so dilutes the solution from which the salt is to be obtained, that evaporation necessary for accuracy requires a great deal of time. The better way is to make such an amount of hot saturated solution by another method that the required weight of the desired salt will crystallize out in cooling from 

100° C. to 20° C., or room temperature. 

(b) Prepare one of the following salts: (The numerator of the fraction shows number of grams of salt soluble in 100 grams of water at 20° C.; the denominator, in 100 parts of water at

Example.-Prepare 15 grams of sodium nitrate crystals by cooling a saturated solution from 100° C. to 20° C. To prepare the sodium nitrate, let nitric acid interact with sodium carbonate. 

Procedure.-178 grams of NaNO, dissolve in 100 cc. of water at 100º C., and 88 grams dissolve in 100 cc. of water at 20° C.; then, in cooling the saturated solution (185 cc.) from 100° C. to 

20° C., 178–88, or 90 grams, would crystallize out. Thus, if we wished to prepare 90 g. of NaNO3, we should simply have to make 185 cc. of saturated solution at 100° C. and then cool the solution to 20° C. 

To prepare 15 g. we shall need to prepare only 15/90 of 

185 cc., or about 31 cc., of saturated solution at 100° C. In 

185 cc. of hot saturated solution there are 178 g. dissolved; then, in 1 cc. there is 0.96 g. and in 31 cc. there are 29.67 g. dissolved.

Therefore, we shall need to form 29.67 g. of NaNO, to have 15 g. crystallize out in cooling from 100° C. to 20° C.

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

Na2CO3 106. g. + 2HNO3 2 X 63 126 g. → H2O + CO2 + 2NaNO3 

29.67 g. x g. 106 

170 29.67 126 

170 29.67 y g. .. x 18.5 g. of Na2CO3. .. y = 24.9 g. of HNO3 (pure). 

Since 6N nitric acid has a density of 1.16 and contains 31 per cent of HNO3, 1 cc. of the acid contains 0.3596 g. (1.16 X 

0.31) of HNO3. It follows from this, that 69 cc. of 6N acid contain 24.9 g. of HNO, (24.9 0.3596).

Weigh out 18.5 g. of Na₂CO, and add it slowly to 69 cc. of boiling 6N nitric acid, stirring all the while. When all the salt has dissolved, wet a glass rod with nitric acid, and dip it into the solution. If effervescence occurs, add nitric acid, drop by drop, until the solution is slightly acid.

Filter the solution and evaporate it to a volume of 31 cc. and set it away to cool. When it is cold, pour off the mother liquor and dry the crystals by leaving them between discs of filter paper over night, then weigh them, and compare with the desired weight.

EXERCISE 39 SODIUM CARBONATE BY THE SOLVAY PROCESS 

(a) Place 50 cc. of a solution of ammonium carbonate. (This solution is on the side shelf and is prepared by dissolving, without the aid of heat, 360 grams of ammonium carbonate in 

1260 cc. of water to which 360 cc. of concentrated ammonium hydroxide have been added.) In a small bottle, add 15 grams of finely divided sodium chloride, cork the bottle and shake thoroughly until no more salt will dissolve. The solution of ammonium carbonate should be thoroughly saturated with sodium chloride. Filter the liquid into another small bottle fitted with a cork and two tubes, one of which reaches to the bottom. Through the latter tube, pass in carbon dioxide until the solution is saturated. This may require an hour or more. 

The carbon dioxide should be washed by passing it through a bottle containing water. After the gas has passed through the solution long enough to expel the air from the bottle, close the exit tube to prevent waste of gas. Shake the bottle occasionally as the carbon dioxide is passed through. When a fair amount of crystals has formed, collect the deposit on a filter, and dry by pressing between sheets of filter paper. EXERCISE 42 STRONTIUM CHLORIDE FROM STRONTIUM SULFATE 

Dissolve about a gram of strontium nitrate in 50 cc. of water, heat to boiling, and add dilute sulfuric slowly and with constant stirring until precipitation is complete. Allow the precipitate to settle and then pour off the liquid. Wash the residue three times by decantation, using about 50 cc. of hot water each time. 

Dissolve about 1.5 grams of anhydrous sodium carbonate in 

8-10 cc. of water, add it to the strontium sulfate, and introduce the suspension into a test tube. Boil, with shaking, for about 

10 minutes, adding a few drops of water now and then to take the place of that lost by evaporation. Allow the precipitate to settle, pour off the supernatant liquid, and then wash the residue three times by decantation, using portions of about 25 cc. of water. Next transfer the residue to a filter and wash with hot water until a fresh portion of the filtrate is no longer alkaline (test?). Finally, dissolve the residue in the smallest possible quantity of dilute hydrochloric, and evaporate the liquid on a clock glass (steam bath) to dryness. Dissolve residue in water and divide the solution into three portions. Test one portion with a solution of silver nitrate, another with dilute sulfuric acid, and the third with ammonium carbonate. Explain.

Note.-The reaction just studied is a reversible one: 

SrSO+ Na2CO3 SrCO3 + Na2SO4. 

The solubility of SrSO, in water is greater than that of SrCO3, So the former is converted into the latter by boiling with a concentrated solution of Na2CO3. This is an excellent reaction to illustrate the principle of solubility-product, or ion product constant. (R 254.) The solubilities in water of these two salts of strontium are as follows: 192

STRONTIUM CHLORIDE 

193 

SrSO 4. 

SrCO 3.. 

Solubility in Grams

Molar Solubility per Liter 0.11 per Liter 0.0006 0.011 0.00007 ×

The solubility-product of SrSO, for a saturated solution is- [Sr++] x [SO-] = Constant = 0.0006 0.0006;

and for SrCO3, [Sr++] x [CO3-] = 0.00007 X 0.00007. 

When strontium sulfate is treated with a concentrated solution of sodium carbonate, the solution soon becomes supersaturated with respect to SrCO, owing to the low ion-product constant. SrCO3, therefore, separates out and the more soluble SrSO4 goes into solution to restore equilibrium. When the two solids are in equilibrium, the ratio of the concentrations of their anions conc. CO 1 

0.0006 0.0006 for conc. SO, 74 0.00007 X 0.00007 1 = 74. In case the concentration ratio is greater than solid SrSO, will be 74' converted into solid SrCO3. (For the preparation of strontium chloride in quantity from strontium sulfate, consult Synthetic Inorganic Chemistry by 

Blanchard and Phelan, pp. 169-172.)

The strontium-ion, Sr++, is colorless and it forms practically insoluble salts with the carbonate-ion and the phosphate-ion.

EXERCISE 43 CUPROUS CHLORIDE 

Dissolve in an Erlenmeyer flask 11 g. of cupric chloride (CuCl₂ 2H2O) in 25 cc. of water and an equal volume of conc. hydrochloric acid. Add about 6 g. of copper turnings and suspend a short-stemmed funnel in the neck of the flask to reduce the loss of hydrochloric acid when the flask is heated. Heat the liquid just to the boiling point until the green color has disappeared; the liquid may become colorless or deep brown. Pour a little of the liquid into a test tube of freshly boiled water; if the latter does not turn blue, the cupric chloride has been completely reduced. Place some asbestos fiber in a funnel, moisten it with conc. hydrochloric acid, and filter the liquid into a bottle or tall beaker containing about 500 cc. of water. When the precipitate has settled, pour off the supernatant liquid, and wash the cuprous chloride by stirring it up with very dilute hydrochloric acid (1 cc. of conc. hydrochloric acid to 100 cc. of water).

Allow the precipitate to settle once more, then pour off the clear liquid. The precipitate may now be collected on a filter and further purified by washing with very dilute hydrochloric acid. 

The drying of the compound may be hastened by washing it, first with alcohol and then with ether. After the latter has drained off, the precipitate is broken up and spread on filter paper to dry. When dry, weigh it on the rough balance. 

Treat a little of the compound with conc. hydrochloric acid.

Explain. Add a solution of sodium hydroxide to alkaline reaction and explain what you observe.

("Scrubber") is filled half full of water. The tower containing calcium oxide may be termed the "lime purifier." Make sure that the apparatus is gas tight, then heat the coal, gradually raising the temperature to the full heat of the burner. 

After all air is expelled from the apparatus, collect some of the gas over water and ignite it, noting the color of the flame. 

Test with neutral litmus paper the contents of the bottles containing the liquids. Also note odor and general character of the liquids. State what you can about their composition and mention the most important substances obtained from them, stating how they are obtained. What are their uses?

Examine the residue left in the tube in which the coal was heated. What are the chief products from the destructive distillation of wood?

Note.-The" ammoniacal liquor" contains ammonium salts such as the sulfide, carbonate, sulfite, and thiosulfate; it may also contain some ammonia. By distilling it with lime, free ammonia is expelled which is readily converted into ammonium salts by passing it into hydrochloric acid or sulfuric acid.

The coal gas, after ordinary purification, consists of a mixture of gases: (1) illuminants, principally unsaturated hydrocarbons such as ethylene, acetylene, and benzene; (2) heating gases (diluents), principally hydrogen, methane, and carbon monoxide; (3) impurities such as nitrogen, carbon dioxide, oxygen, and carbon disulfide. (R 731-736.)

What is the composition of the final solid substance? (c) Thoroughly clean a test tube by boiling dilute sodium hydroxide in it and then rinsing it out with nitric acid, followed several times with water. Pour about 10 cc. of dilute silver nitrate solution into the tube and add ammonium hydroxide drop by drop with shaking until the precipitate which first forms just goes into solution. Add a small amount of a solution of glucose and heat the test tube in hot water. What is deposited on the tube? Glucose reduces the ammoniacal solution of silver nitrate.

Note.-Glucose is converted by oxidizing agents, first into gluconic acid and then into saccharic acid. No equations are required. (d) Dissolve about 0.1 g. of glucose in 10 cc. of water, then put exactly 10 cc. of Fehling's solution (R 747) into a clean test tube, and add a few drops of the sugar solution to the 

Fehling solution and boil. (Do not spill any of the liquid.) 

Add more of the glucose solution and boil again. Continue to do this until the blue color disappears. It requires 0.005 g. of glucose to reduce 1 cc. of Fehling's solution.

Note.-Fehling's solution is utilized to determine the amount of sugar in urine, which sometimes amounts to 8 to 10 per cent in severe cases of diabetes. 215

Benzene and Aniline (a) Heat in a small closed tube a little of a mixture of soda-lime and benzoic acid (C,H,COOH) or sodium benzoate (CH, COONa) and ignite the escaping vapor; observe the character of the flame.

Note. The equation for the formation of benzene may be written thus (see methane): CH, COONa+ NaOH→ C.H. + Na2CO3. 

What is the chief source of benzene? By whom and when was it discovered? Why is it so important? (b) To 2 or 3 drops of benzene in a test tube add an equal volume of strong nitric acid and warm the mixture by immersing the end of the tube in hot water for a short while, then add about 5 cc. of water and shake the tube. Note the odor of the oily liquid; it is nitrobenzene. What is its color?

Note.-Nitrobenzene is usually prepared in the laboratory by slowly adding to benzene a mixture of nitric acid of sp. gr. 1.45 and conc. sulfuric acid, the temperature being kept below 40°:

C.H. + HO-NO,→ CH•NO, + H,O. 

What part does the sulfuric acid play? (R 389[6–8])

Nitrobenzene is a pale-yellow oil having an odor similar to that of benzaldehyde. It is poisonous, heavier than water, and is chiefly used in the manufacture of aniline. (c) To a drop or two of nitrobenzene in a test tube add finely divided zinc and a little dilute sulfuric acid, shake the 225

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

mixture occasionally for some time, filter, and to the filtrate add a little potassium chlorate; note the color. If it does not appear, add a little conc. sulfuric acid very carefully. 

Note.-Aniline (C.H, NH2), the source of innumerable dyes, is produced by reducing nitrobenzene by means of hydrogen. (R 736.) Aniline is poisonous. State other properties of the compound. 

Note that aniline is not a dye, but it may be transformed into dyes by chemical treatment. 

Look up the discovery of "mauve." (R 737.) Dyeing (d) Direct Dyes.-Shake thoroughly a suspension of chrysophenin and add 6 cc. of it to 40 cc. of water contained in an evaporating dish. Now add 2 cc. of a solution of Na₂SO and then heat to the boiling point. Immerse in the liquid a small piece of white flannel and one of unsized cotton; stir the bath for two minutes, then remove the cloth and wash thoroughly with running water. Record the color. Is chrysophenin a direct dye for both wool and cotton? (e) Dyeing and Mordants.-Dilute 2 cc. of a normal solution of aluminium sulfate in a test tube with 10 cc. of water, introduce a piece of white cotton cloth and heat to boiling for 

2 or 3 minutes, then remove the cloth and wring it.

Pour into an evaporating dish 5 cc. of a suspension of alizarin and add 50 cc. of water. Add ammonium hydroxide until the solution is slightly alkaline, introduce the cloth and boil for a minute or two. Rinse thoroughly in distilled water and note whether or not the color is permanent. Repeat the experiment with a piece of cloth not previously treated with the salt of aluminium. What difference do you observe? 

Note. Some dyes (e.g., chrysophenin and indigo) are direct dyes, that is, they are fast on the cloth without the addition of a mordant (Latin mordere, to bite). Chrysophenin is a most important direct yellow dye.

Other dyes, such as alizarin (madder) and malachite green, require a mordant. Thus, in the first part of the experiment just performed, aluminium hydroxide was the mordant; it formed a lake with the alizarin,

AROMATIC COMPOUNDS; DYEING 

227 which is fast on the material, for it cannot be removed by washing. Different lakes have different colors.

EXERCISE 60 TEST OF PURITY OF A SUBSTANCE 

Compounds may be purified by (1) recrystallization; (2) immiscible solvents (R 170); (3) fractional distillation; (4) fractional precipitation; and (5) sublimation. 

Before an organic compound is analyzed, it is customary to check its purity by a determination of either its boiling point or its melting point. A pure substance capable of ebullition without decomposition has a constant boiling point. Also, pure substances which fuse without decomposition have definite melting points. (a) Boiling Point. The boiling point may be determined in a distillation flask, fitted with a thermometer as shown in Fig. 

12. Heat is applied, and as soon as the mercurial column is constant, the temperature is noted. 

Determine in a 50 cc. distillation flask the boiling point of absolute alcohol, using about 25 cc. of alcohol and a small free flame. The bulb of the thermometer is placed just below the side tube of the flask. Note the barometric pressure.

Note. It is well known that the boiling point rises with increase in pressure.

In very accurate work a correction is made for the mercury thread exposed to the air, or not exposed to the vapor of the substance being boiled. (b) Melting Point. The melting point of a solid may be determined by means of the apparatus shown in Fig. 28. Take a piece of thin quill tubing 4-5 mm. in diameter and draw it out into a capillary about 1-1.5 mm. in external diameter. Cut this into tubes about 6 cm. long, and close by fusion one end 228

TEST OF PURITY OF A SUBSTANCE 

229 of each tube. Introduce into the tube a very small portion of the substance, the melting point of which is to be determined. 

The tube is now fastened by means of a small rubber ring to the bulb of the thermometer.

Suspend the whole in a liquid which has a boiling point higher than the melting point of the solid. Heat the liquid gently and stir it continuously by means of a twisted glass rod. Note the temperature just as soon as the substance melts.

By repeating the experiment two or three times, a fair average is obtained. 

Determine the melting point of chloral hydrate, CCI, CH(OH)2. Since it is easily -Stirring Rod -Thermometer Bulb Solid

FIG. 28. 

fused, water may be used as a bath. Also, determine the melting point of naphthalene (C₁0H8). 10 For certain substances, such as urea, glucose and cane sugar, which melt above the boiling point of water, a bath of conc. sulfuric acid may be employed. Determine the melting point of urea.

EXERCISE 61 IDENTIFICATION OF CONSTITUENTS OF CARBON COMPOUNDS 

(a) Carbon.-(1) Heat in a closed tube a little starch or sugar, noting what you observe. The formation of CO₂ may be detected by means of limewater. (2) Repeat the experiment, using benzoic acid. (3) Also, heat a little anhydrous sodium acetate, and after the residue is cool add a drop or two of acetic acid. Explain. 

Account for the presence of K₂CO, in wood ashes. 

Note.-Carbon compounds, as a rule, char, or yield carbon, when suddenly brought to a high temperature. Some carbon compounds (e.g., benzoic acid) undergo sublimation. Organic salts of alkali and of alkaline-earth metals form carbonates. (b) Nitrogen. (1) Support in the vertical position a clean, dry, hard or Pyrex glass test tube about 10 cm. X 1 cm. This may be done by means of a clamp, or the tube may be pushed to its rim through a hole in a small square of asbestos cardboard resting on the ring of a retort stand. Cut a piece of metallic sodium equal in size to a cube about 3 mm. on each edge, and remove all oil by means of filter paper. The metal should have fresh clean surfaces. Melt the sodium by means of a small blue flame until it begins to vaporize, then drop into the tube from the point of a knife blade about 0.1 g. of thiourea, SC(NH2)2, so that it falls directly into the molten metal, and continue to heat the tube until the reaction appears to be over, finally heating the mass to redness for about a minute, and then allow the tube to cool to room temperature. Next, add a few drops of alcohol to dissolve excess of sodium, using a glass rod to break up the mass. When effervescence ceases, cautiously add 2 or 3 drops of distilled water. When the reaction is over, 230

CONSTITUENTS OF CARBON COMPOUNDS 

231 add about 5 cc. of water, heat, and filter through a wet filter paper, and then rinse the tube two or three times with a little hot water, making the total volume about 10 cc. The filtrate should be colorless if the decomposition has been satisfactorily accomplished. If not, repeat the experiment. In case the solution is not already alkaline, render it so by adding sodium hydroxide. Portions of this solution are to be tested for nitrogen and sulfur. (2) To 3 cc. of the solution prepared in (1) add 5 drops of freshly prepared ferrous sulfate solution and a little potassium fluoride, and then stopper the tube and rotate it just enough to mix the substances. Set the test tube aside for 5-10 minutes and then acidify the liquid with sulfuric acid of approximately normal strength. The formation of Prussian blue shows the presence of nitrogen. 

Another portion of the solution may be tested for sulfur as directed below.

Note. When a carbon compound containing nitrogen is heated with an alkali metal, a cyanide of the metal is formed, which can be transformed into Prussian blue. In some way not definitely understood potassium fluoride aids in the precipitation of Prussian blue. (3) Heat a mixture of gelatine and soda-lime in a closed tube. Is ammonia formed?

Note. In many cases nitrogen compounds yield ammonia when heated with soda-lime. (c) Chlorine.-Fix a small lump of cupric oxide in a platinum wire, strongly ignite it, then dip it in chloroform (CHCl₁), and again ignite, first in the inner, and then in the outer, flame; note the color of the flame. 

Note. A green flame indicates a halogen compound.

Halogen compounds may also be detected by igniting the compound with soda-lime in a combustion tube closed at one end; the contents are then dissolved in nitric acid and treated with silver nitrate. (d) Sulfur.—(1) Mix about 1 g. of anhydrous sodium carbonate with 0.5 g. of potassium nitrate and fuse the mixture in

LABORATORY COURSE OF GENERAL CHEMISTRY 

a porcelain crucible. Carefully add, little by little, about 0.1 g. of thiourea, SC (NH2)2, and continue to heat until the action appears to be over; cool the melt, dissolve it in dilute hydrochloric acid, filter if not clear, and add a solution of barium chloride. A ppt. of barium sulfate indicates sulfur. (2) Heat a small amount of thiourea with 5 cc. of a solution of sodium hydroxide to which a few drops of a solution of lead acetate have been added. A brownish-black ppt. of lead sulfide should be obtained. 

Test, also, egg albumin for sulfur. 

Note. In many cases, upon boiling a carbon compound containing sulfur, with an alkaline solution of lead oxide, lead sulfide is formed. (3) To 2 cc. of the solution prepared under Nitrogen (b, 1) add a few drops of a freshly prepared solution of sodium nitroprusside. A violet coloration shows the presence of sulfur. (e) Phosphorus.-Fuse egg albumin or yolk of egg with sodium carbonate and potassium nitrate, as under sulfur (d, 1); treat the cooled product with nitric acid, filter if not clear, then add two volumes of ammonium molybdate, heat to a temperature which can be just borne with the hand, and allow to stand. 

A yellow ppt. indicates phosphoric acid, formed by the oxidation of phosphorus.

Note.-In case phosphorus is present in nitrogen compounds fused with sodium (see (b), above), it may be detected by boiling some of the solution with conc. nitric acid and then adding ammonium molybdate. Nitric acid oxides phosphide to phosphate. 

How may hydrogen be detected in a carbon compound? 

Problems.-(1) 0.481 g. of urea, when burnt in a stream of oxygen, yielded 0.2918 g. of H₂O and 0.363 g. of CO,. Calculate the percentage of hydrogen and of carbon in the urea. (2) 0.300 g. of the urea was ignited with soda-lime, and the 

NH, evolved neutralized 10 cc. of N sulfuric acid. Find the percentage of nitrogen in the specimen of urea. (3) Urea contains oxygen along with carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen. Making use of the results found in (1) and (2),

CONSTITUENTS OF CARBON COMPOUNDS 

233 find the percentage of oxygen by difference, and then derive the simplest formula for urea. (4) 12 g. of urea were dissolved in 100 g. of water and the freezing point of the solution was found to be -3.7° C. Calculate the molecular weight of urea. (R 177.) What is its molecular formula? (See problem [3].)

APPENDIX 

A. On being assigned to a desk a student has the privilege, before signing the list, of returning any of the articles the desk may contain.

B. Students must see that every article is in satisfactory condition when received; no apparatus can be afterwards exchanged for any reason whatever.

C. As soon as the laboratory work in this course is completed, students should secure a copy of this apparatus list from the storeroom. When the regulations for cleaning and checking the equipment have been complied with, credit will be given as stated under each class of articles. 

D. In order to receive full credit all equipment must be cleaned and checked before the beginning of the Final Examination Period of the term unless additional work is to be done in the laboratory.

Regulations Governing Checking of Desks 

The following rules must be observed in order to receive credit: 

1. Clean all apparatus as directed under Method of Cleaning as no item will be accepted unless clean and in good condition. 

2. Return to the storeroom all apparatus which does not appear on the list so that you will receive credit for those items which have been charged to you. 

3. Secure apparatus from storekeeper to replace all "First 

Class" items which are broken or missing. 

4. When your equipment is ready, have your instructor sign the slip stating that you have completed your laboratory course and that it is in order for the storekeeper to check your apparatus.

Method of Checking 

1. Spread out your apparatus on your desk before the appointed time for checking, and then notify the storekeeper that it is ready to be checked.

APPENDIX 

237 2. As the storekeeper checks off the various items, arrange the first class apparatus in your desk in the order in which they are named. 

3. As third class items cannot be accepted for credit, it is advisable to keep them for use in your next course. 

4. As soon as your apparatus has been verified and accepted, lock your desk and give the key to the storekeeper. 

Method of Cleaning 

All glassware and porcelain must be free from dirt, stains, and carbon deposits. Unless apparatus is thoroughly cleaned and dried, no credit will be given. 

1. Secure from the storeroom a bottle of "Dichromate Cleaning Solution." As this solution is more efficient when hot, heat the contents of the bottle in your largest beaker. After rinsing the article to be cleansed with water to remove all free deposits, partly fill the article with the cleaning solution and gently agitate. When the adhering substances have been loosened, remove the cleaning solution and rinse with clear water. Dry all accessible surfaces with a clean towel. Do not throw away the cleaning solution. Either pour it back into a beaker or into the next article to be cleansed. Remove test tubes from support, and wipe off dirt and chemical deposits from the support. After test tubes have been cleansed and dried, replace them in rack. 

When all the glassware has been cleansed, pour the solution into the bottle and return to storeroom. As this is a corrosive acid solution, take care not to get it on hands or clothing. 

2. Carbon deposits may be removed from porcelain by moistening a portion of your sponge with Ammonium Hydroxide and dipping in sand, then rubbing the surface to be cleansed.

IV. CONCENTRATION OF REAGENTS 

Acids Acetic 

17 N: Use commercial glacial acetic acid, about 99.5% acid, sp. gr. 1.055. 

6N: Mix 350 cc. of 99.5% acid with 650 cc. of water.

Hydrochloric 

12 N: Use commercial concentrated acid, sp. gr. 1.19. 

6N: Dilute the 12 N acid with an equal volume of water. Nitric 

16 N: Use commercial concentrated nitric acid, sp. gr. 1.42. 

6 N: Mix 3 volumes of the 16 N acid with 5 volumes of water. Sulphuric 

36 N: Use commercial concentrated acid, about 95% acid, sp. gr. 1.84. 

6 N: Pour 1 volume of the 95% acid into 5 volumes of water.

APPENDIX 

243 

Bases Ammonium Hydroxide 

15 N: Use commercial concentrated solution, sp. gr. 0.90.

Dilute 2 volumes of the 15 N solution with 3 volumes of water. 

Sodium Hydroxide 

6 N: Dissolve 250 grams of NaOH in water and dilute to 

1000 cc. Salts

Normal salt solutions are very satisfactory unless solutions of special concentrations are called for.

IX. DEGREE OF IONIZATION OF ACIDS, BASES, AND SALTS 

The fraction of an acid, base, or salt ionized depends, according to Arrhenius, primarily upon the dilution of the solution and upon the nature of the substance.
